7 Procurement strategies and
procurement routes
Introduction
It is probable that, in the UK, the dominance of the public sector in the 1950s and
1960s in terms of capital spend established an approach to building procurement
which became accepted as the norm. Design was completed for the planned project
by a selected architect prior to tender documents being prepared and bids sought
based upon those documents, usually from a selected list of contractors. In most
cases the tender documents included a bill of quantities prepared by a quantity
surveyor, identifying precisely the extent of work to be done for a lump sum bid.
Until the 1980s the selection of architects and quantity surveyors was based upon
reputation with fee scales being mandatory but, once these were outlawed, the
selection of consultants was also based upon submitted bids.
This process adopted the nomenclature ‘traditional procurement’ and was for
many years the mainstay of procurement activity, the default approach, but latterly
the traditional approach has become less appropriate for some projects with the
advancement of integrated approaches including design and build, management
contracting and construction management.
Whilst the established construction professions have co-operated in discussions
about change, the primary drivers in achieving change appear to have arisen as the
result of client pressure caused by increasing frustration at the apparent inability of
the construction industry as a whole to deliver the projects needed on time, to
budget and to acceptable quality standards.
Consequently there is evidence over the past two decades of some change in the
procurement of construction projects in the UK, but this has been relatively slow
and seemingly driven by a combination of major private sector clients and central
government.
The latest data available to indicate the relative usage of different procurement
strategies show that in overall terms there has been a significant reduction in the
use of Bills of Quantity in recent years. The Royal Institution of Chartered Surveyors
(RICS 2010) states that in 2007 (the latest year for which data is available):

-- 128 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	111
The use of Bills of Quantities dropped to its lowest level in the survey’s
history. Firm BQs were used on 20% of contracts in the sample. . . . By value,
the proportion of contracts using firm BQs dropped to just 13.2% – the first
time this measure has dropped below 20%.
In contrast, RICS (2010) shows that the use of design and build procurement
appears to be rising fast, at least in terms of the number of projects:
The design and build route has shown a significant increase. . . . 21.8% of all
contracts used a D&B route, up from 13.3% in the 2004 survey and the
highest figure so far recorded in the survey’s history. . . . However, in value
terms, 32.6% of the sample employed a D&B route, which was the lowest
level since 1995.
There appears to be little general movement in terms of other procurement
approaches, except that it appears likely that, from 1998, government pressure led
to an increase in the number of projects procured through collaborative routes
such as partnering. In 2011 the UK Government published a construction strategy
(Cabinet Office 2011) which appears to favour framework agreements and a guar-
anteed maximum price approach for public sector projects.
There is also evidence of the increasing adoption in the UK of projects procured
through franchising opportunities to capture income flow, such as Private Finance
Initiative (PFI) schemes, driven no doubt by government’s view over the past dec-
ade that every major project should be tested for suitability as a PFI scheme before
any other procurement routes are examined. PFI and public private partnership
(PPP) are considered in detail in Chapters 13 and 14.
In a telling interview given to the RICS Business magazine (RICS 2003), Lord
Digby Jones, Director General of the Confederation of British Industry (CBI),
commented that the construction industry:
is not yet exploiting its chance to reinvent itself and become an example to
the rest of business . . . [it] talks the talk about change but walking the walk is
a lot more difficult. And because it’s such a disparate business full of self-
employed, temporary staff and conservative people, change is exceedingly
hard. It brings insecurity and a feeling of isolation.
On the basis of the collected data this appears to continue to be the position at
the time of writing and change is still difficult to achieve. In procurement terms
changes which have occurred mainly affect the way that capital projects are pur-
chased. Traditional approaches reflected a time when the public purse dominated
construction expenditure – they facilitated excellent accountability and relative
price certainty. Deterioration of standards seems to have become an acceptable
reality, with bills of quantities being prepared and often deemed to be ‘firm quanti-
ties’ in contractual terms when often in reality they are not (Kings 2002). This may
be because of the incomplete state of design when detailed drawings are required
but at the same time clients are pressing for earlier and earlier starts on site with
faster and faster completion. Extensive post-contract changes also appear to have

-- 129 of 346 --

112 	Chapter 7
become the norm. RICS has now published new rules of measurement (RICS
2012a, 2012b) which may, as they become generally adopted, instigate change and
engender a more positive attitude to the value of measurement in the procurement
process.
A move to non-traditional approaches that are faster but give some level of price
and time certainty was perhaps inevitable given the decreasing confidence created
by lower standards, particularly at periods of buoyant economic growth or strong
property markets. This may not, however, be in the long-term interests of those
clients whose primary criteria or objectives are related to building performance
or functionality.
Egan (1998, 2002) argued that integrating design and construction would facili-
tate improved value in projects for clients. Whilst it is probably true that an increase
in the use of design and build will enable integrated design and construction,
whether this will translate into improved value for money or functional perfor-
mance is a matter of judgement over time. The shift to design and build also means
a shift of the responsibility for the design function away from direct client control
to organisations whose core businesses are profit-focused.
Other ways to achieve integration seem to generate less enthusiasm from the
client sector or the construction professions, as evidenced by the very low adoption
of strategies such as management contracting and construction management.
According to the latest available statistics (RICS 2010), in 2007 construction
management contracts accounted for only 1.1% of the number of contracts awarded
in the UK (9.6% of the total value of contracts awarded), with management
contracting accounting for 0.7% and 1% respectively.
The construction industry is complex and the demand for construction is
variable and fickle, with an experienced minority of clients accounting for most of
the expenditure and an inexperienced majority accounting for least spend and
most projects. The experienced group are able to influence and encourage change
using the advantage of their spending power, while the inexperienced group are
often at the mercy of construction professionals who have historically appeared
largely to resist change and to defend traditional practices.
These problems are compounded by the fact that the supply side of the construc-
tion industry is fragmented in terms of both design and construction. Advances in
technology have resulted in specialisation structured in such a way that traditional
practices often do not enable specialist designers and contractors to contribute to
the design, costing and programming elements of a project. For example, those
involved in constructing the specialist elements of a building, often very significant
elements, frequently do not become involved until so late in the process that their
ability to contribute is severely curtailed.
This is counter to the way most integrated manufacturing industry processes
work, but the construction industry has historically displayed few characteristics
similar to manufacturing industry, being mainly project-focused rather than pro-
cess-focused. There are, however, indications that those collaborative procurement
practices which have been adopted along with improved supply chain management
processes, such as partnering, slowly change and improve the way things are done.

-- 130 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	113
A further example is the development of Building Information Modelling (BIM).
An integrated database of coordinated information using digital technology
requires the contribution of all participants in the project process. Thus the design
element of a building is supplied by CAD by all those involved and is held in one
place and coordinated along with other relevant data. The extent to which it will
enable contribution towards a fully integrated resource will depend upon the skills
and abilities of the participants.
BIM has perceived advantages for clients in the design of buildings, particularly
the saving of time and money, reducing claims and increasing the quality of
performance. It should enable a coordinated approach to design and ensure an
ongoing record of the as-built structure. It is created at an early stage in terms of
general acceptance and as such it also carries risk and uncertainty in terms of the
liabilities and duties of the many participants in construction. Errors or omissions
can prove a minefield.
BIM will provide a platform for new duties for consultants and contractors in
terms of procurement, but as yet the representative bodies are still working on what
duties and how contracts will change to accommodate BIM. Few are BIM ready.
The intellectual property rights and liabilities which go with them can get confused
so the difficulties under current standard contracts are enormous and will require
change. Project managers may assume the role of BIM manager ensuring that the
model is working but not as owner of the intellectual rights of the contributors.
The government’s construction strategy (Cabinet Office 2011) acknowledged
that at the industry’s leading edge there are companies which have the capability of
working in a fully 3D environment with a shared platform with reduced transac-
tion costs and less opportunity for error. But it recognises that construction has
lagged behind other industries in the adoption of the full potential offered by
digital technology. In highlighting a lack of compatible systems, standards and
protocols and the differing requirements of clients and lead designers as a primary
cause of inhibition, it has acknowledged that the implication of alternative design
proposals cannot be evaluated with any form of certainty or ease. The move of
RICS in developing a suite of rules for measurement is plainly a move in the right
direction (see also Chapter 4).
However, with a few exceptions the team constructed to design and construct a
project is temporary, and projects have therefore been largely unable to benefit
from established supply chains or prototype testing. There are indications that this
too is changing. The increasing use of long-term collaborative arrangements and
an increase in the use of multi-project framework agreements, again largely but not
exclusively driven by central government, is beginning to change the way continu-
ous development is viewed.
Comparison of the industry key performance indicators (KPIs) with perceived
performance over the last five years (Wilén 2010) shows some improvement in
client satisfaction, industry productivity and time predictability, although cost
predictability remains disappointingly poor. Client dissatisfaction in the private
sector may result from the fact that most clients (by number) are inexperienced
and have unrealistic expectations of an industry of which they are largely unaware.

-- 131 of 346 --

114 	Chapter 7
These expectations are often not discouraged by a project team setting targets with
little or no involvement of those specialists whose work will have a major effect on
costing and programming. Alternatively perceived poor performance may result
from design teams agreeing to unreasonable client targets in ignorance, or in order
to encourage the client to progress, or an acceptance of such targets by construction
companies unwilling to point out unrealistic outcomes, if that may result in losing
the opportunity to obtain the contract.
There is clearly a remaining challenge to seeking further improvement. As
observed above, however, the construction industry is still largely project-focused,
not process-driven, and whilst desirable, the opportunities to adopt and benefit
from collaborative techniques are probably limited to those clients who have a con-
tinuing relationship with particular constructors, specialists or design teams. The
majority of clients, who buy one-off projects occasionally, are less likely to benefit
from the adoption of such techniques. Other strategies will need to be developed to
improve perceived construction performance in this demand sector.
The Construction Clients’ Forum (CCF) (latterly the Confederation of
Construction Clients and now the Construction Clients Group) published, as
long ago as 1998, a document which included a suggested ‘pact’ with the construc-
tion industry, setting down what was felt to be a way forward (CCF 1998). This
‘pact’ sought improvement from the industry by suggesting that the construction
industry:
■	■ 	Present clients with objective and appropriate advice on the options and choices
to meet their needs.
■	■ 	Introduce a ‘right first time’ culture, with the projects finished on time and to
budget.
■	■ 	Eliminate waste, streamline processes and work towards continuous improve-
ment.
■	■ 	Work towards standardisation in components where this provides efficiency
gains.
■	■ 	Use a properly trained and certificated workforce and keep skills up to date.
■	■ 	Improve the management of supply chains.
■	■ 	Keep abreast of changing technology by innovation and investment in research
and development.
An objective view of these demands from customers in other industries would
probably conclude that they are not unreasonable.
Clients, on the other hand, were to accept as part of the ‘pact’ that they had
responsibility to become ‘intelligent clients’, and since then the concept of a client’s
charter has progressed, even if it is only understood and progressed by experienced
clients.
Most of the focus, then, over the last few years has been on strategic issues with
the industry and its professionals ‘talking the talk’. There is indeed (as Digby Jones
suggested (RICS 2003)) much less evidence of them ‘walking the walk’ in relation
to the adoption of improved strategies, as indicated by the actual strategies adopted
and the relatively low use of innovative processes or products.

-- 132 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	115
Progress can be achieved and measured through the implementation of known
tools and techniques. Where and when measured improvements follow they will be
used again. Typical examples may include:
■	■ 	The adoption of simple techniques to enable project objectives to be prioritised
and client needs and risks to be clearly defined.
■	■ 	An increased focus on preplanning to reduce late changes.
■	■ 	The adoption of value management at an early stage.
■	■ 	A balance between capital cost and long-term ‘worth’ in terms of the business
case for the project.
■	■ 	An increase in the early appointment of specialists through collaborative strat-
egies to enable their input into design and costing.
Such techniques can very quickly improve predictability and value for money.
Their adoption moves aspiration towards implementation. One key area identified
by clients (CCF 1998) was that they did not like unpleasant surprises, although
there remains clear evidence from industry-wide KPIs that these still occur.
In summary the industry is unique and complex. It is predominantly project-
focused in producing its output but frequently seeks to adopt process-manufacturing
terminology, often in an inappropriate way. Whilst clients usually benefit signifi-
cantly from their completed process, performance is often seen to be sub-optimal.
Whilst this may not be a true reflection of the industry’s recent achievements,
opportunities for improvement in procurement processes do exist, particularly by
increasing the objective advice available for clients and the adoption of simple tech-
niques already available within the sector.
Other improvements cannot simply be achieved through the application of
techniques and this must be trusted to the representative groups and institutions at
the heart of the industry. Although there has been some acceptance in the UK of
so-called modern methods of construction, many still continue to design buildings
requiring traditional skills whilst at the same time observing a constant reduction
in the availability of skilled tradesmen and training places. Innovative processes
and products may be an alternative solution if the skilled workforce continues to
shrink, but investment in research and development in the construction industry is
still only about 10% of that of other industries. The innovative products and
processes available are resisted by more-conservative designers and professionals
and appear to be less popular with real-estate funders.
These and more strategic procurement issues may be resolved over time. In the
meantime clients will expect some of the improvements that are achievable by the
professional application of available techniques in the procurement of their projects.
Procurement strategy
A construction project is one way of delivering a solution to the particular business
needs of clients, whether for investment, expansion or improved efficiency. When
new build solutions are selected, rather than renting, leasing or purchasing existing

-- 133 of 346 --

116 	Chapter 7
real estate, there is usually the need for a bespoke solution which aims to meet
particular objectives.
New build projects are frequently complex one-off projects with unique designs
on unique sites, and a successful outcome is only achievable only where the
complexity of the processes involved are recognised and addressed appropriately.
Unlike the processes adopted in manufacturing, construction activities are not an
ongoing production line and the management of projects requires different skills.
The team drawn together for the project will disperse at its completion and members
are unlikely to form the same team again. If they do, the project will be different.
Design is largely segregated from the project construction process which itself
will need to integrate specialists from a wide range of fields of activity.
In such a scenario the role of the client is vital and includes developing a strategic
brief for the project based upon a functional needs analysis and rooted in a clearly
established business case. Also the client must ensure that necessary decisions are
made and resources are available throughout the project. Procuring construction as
a business solution can involve high risk to the client organisation. That risk will
require managing and will involve client intervention in both strategic and imple-
mentation terms, usually with the assistance of a team of construction professionals.
Most clients will want to ensure as far as possible from the outset that they can
achieve the solution they require within affordable cost and by an acceptable date
in the future. This will be best achieved if the client seeks independent advice on
these matters from the outset from an experienced construction professional, argu-
ably one who will not become part of the final project team, and who can therefore
perhaps adopt a more objective viewpoint. Where, in meeting the needs of the
business case, there is particular focus upon building function or running costs,
speed to completion or capital cost, an appropriate strategy for procuring the build-
ing while reflecting this focus must be adopted.
The establishment of a procurement strategy that identifies and prioritises key
project objectives as well as reflecting aspects of risk, and establishing how the
team will be selected and how the process will be managed, is vital to a successful
outcome. On a procedural front, and with the intention of standardising processes,
the British Standards Institution has published a new British Standard for procure-
ment, BS 8534, aimed at procurement on a strategic level, seeking to collect in one
document all the guidance towards best practice that has been issued (BSI 2012).
The unique and bespoke nature of most construction projects does increase the
inherent risks. These risks include completing a project that does not meet the
functional needs of the business; a project that is delivered later than the initial
programme; or a project that costs more than the client’s ability to pay or fund. All
of these risks could potentially have a high impact on the client’s core business, and
a procurement strategy should be developed that balances the risks against the
project objectives established at an early stage.
Most clients will want a high-quality functional building, fast, for a low price.
This is rarely, if ever, achievable since it is likely that these requirements are often
in conflict with each other, and unless there is a priority of criteria, disappointment
or worse may follow. The nature of the client’s business and the business case

-- 134 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	117
underpinning the project will enable consideration to be given to which of the
criteria – time certainty, price or function – is of the greatest importance. The
identification of which factor will constitute the greatest risk to the business if it
fails to be achieved will assist in the development of a weighted list of priorities.
The strategic brief will enable the development of a procurement strategy for the
project that establishes how the team will be selected, how the project will be designed
and what the parameters are; the project execution plan (PEP) will define how project
delivery will be implemented. Because the strategy is based upon the unique needs of
the client, the key parameters will be clearly communicated to the project team. A key
role for the client is to ensure that the strategy established at the outset is not lost sight
of as the immediate priorities of the design and construction processes are progressed.
The PEP is a key document in ensuring that decisions made at this stage are embed-
ded in the progress of the project. This is referred to in some detail in Chapter 6.
Establishment of an appropriate project team to deliver the right project at the
right time for the right cost given the adopted strategy is a vital role for the client,
who again should take independent advice. In the selection process better out-turns
are usually experienced where selection is based upon quality as well as price.
Where the running costs of the building or the design itself are of particular
complexity or importance, procurement routes enabling the maximum amount of
collaboration within the project team are desirable.
The role of the client in construction is a vital one to success and involves a num-
ber of functions, which most clients have to adopt infrequently:
■	■ 	Developing a vision and confirming its viability.
■	■ 	Funding a single project throughout its progress.
■	■ 	Buying the skills of construction professionals.
■	■ 	Responding to requests for decisions at key stages.
Whilst each of these functions are complex in themselves and will need to be
carried out in parallel with the usual activities of the client organisation, they are
achievable if an appropriate strategy is developed at an early stage and adopted
throughout the project. Such a strategy will encompass a separate selection meth-
odology for designers and for constructors or a selection strategy for companies
who will offer an integrated approach.
Procurement routes
There are procurement routes which can be adopted that will align with the selected
strategy and will achieve:
■	■ 	Certainty of cost and time for a design developed by an architect employed by
the client – but this traditional procurement process is sequential and conse-
quently slow, and is open to disputes as to responsibility if serious defects
become apparent either during construction or after completion.
■	■ 	Relative speed and cost certainty – but the design will be to a greater or lesser
extent the responsibility of a contractor (develop and construct or design and

-- 135 of 346 --

118 	Chapter 7
build) and consequently the client will lose some degree of control over the
design process. These processes will however substantially overcome the prob-
lem of a potentially divided responsibility for defects.
■	■ 	Speedy completion for a design developed by an architect employed by the
client (either management contracting or construction management) – but in
this case cost may be very uncertain until close to completion, and there is little
scope to incorporate client-inspired changes during the construction process.
The procurement route will establish contractual relationships between the par-
ties. The choice of an appropriate procurement route is a key decision if project
success is to be achieved. Mistakes made at this stage may have unfortunate conse-
quences later and it is worth taking time to ensure that the prioritisation of objec-
tives and attitude to project risks are understood and accepted. As soon as possible
after this initial stage the general parameters associated with the project will need
defining, such as time constraints, budgets for all associated costs and issues that
are critical to the suitability and functionality of design.
For complex buildings, and particularly where complex and multiheaded clients
are involved, a successful procurement approach may include the appointment of a
client project sponsor or project director. The client project sponsor needs to be
someone who has a detailed knowledge of the client’s business, and also has some
experience in the management of projects. They will preferably be drawn from
within the client organisation, although where no suitable internal appointment
can be found, a consultant appointment may be the only way forward. Their role
will be to ensure that the client gets the project required. The role of the project
sponsor is therefore to manage the client within the context of the project, to resolve
disputes within the client organisation, and to present a single point of contact to
the construction team. He or she must therefore be invested with sufficient author-
ity to manage the client function on behalf of the client organisation.
The project sponsor will need to consult with all stakeholders at an early stage to
establish the specific functional needs that the project must fulfil (there is an
important distinction to be made here between real needs, that is the things the
project must do, and wants or things that would be nice to have), to develop realis-
tic time and cost budgets against which the client organisation will have to make
ongoing project decisions, and to plan the resourcing and funding of the project
(see Chapter 6). Often, and particularly with complex projects, this function is del-
egated in part or in whole to an experienced project manager, who will see the
project through all its phases from initiation to commissioning.
When the project has been defined, all the factors influencing the project have
been identified and the project requirements analysed, the final strategy for the
project can be developed.
It is likely that there will be more than one route that can match the proposed strat-
egy and can be adopted to achieve the requirements of the project. It is important to
consider carefully each option, as each will address the various influencing factors to a
different extent. In developing strategies, a potential danger is that only the most obvious
course of action may be considered – this is not necessarily the best in the longer term.

-- 136 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	119
Procurement routes will differ from each other in relation to:
■	■ 	The financial risk to which the client is exposed.
■	■ 	The degree of control that the client has over the design and construction
processes.
■	■ 	The extent of design information that will be available at the time of tender.
■	■ 	The information required at the time construction work can commence.
■	■ 	The extent of involvement of the contractor in the design stage when the
contractor may be able to contribute to the design and planning of the project.
■	■ 	The organisational arrangements which distribute risk, responsibility and
accountability.
■	■ 	The sequential character of the process.
Factors to be considered in selecting a procurement route
Once the primary strategy has been established the factors listed below should be
considered when evaluating the most appropriate procurement route:
■	■ 	Factors outside the control of the project team.
■	■ 	Client resources.
■	■ 	Project characteristics.
■	■ 	Need for and the ability to make changes at various stages as the project proceeds.
■	■ 	Risk management.
■	■ 	Cost issues.
■	■ 	Timing.
■	■ 	Quality and performance.
Inevitably, some of these requirements will be in conflict and priorities need to be
decided. The choice of route should ensure that control is maintained over those
factors that are of most importance to the client.
Factors outside the control of the project team
Consideration must be given to the potential impact of economic, commercial,
technological, social, political and legal factors which influence the client and the
project team or are likely to do so during the lifetime of the project. These may
include:
■	■ 	Changes to interest rates where borrowing levels for the project are relatively
high.
■	■ 	Increases in the level of inflation, affecting the products or trades used in the
works.
■	■ 	Changes in the local or national demand for construction, affecting tender
price levels.
■	■ 	Changes to legislation, affecting the design of the works or the methods of
achieving that design.

-- 137 of 346 --

120 	Chapter 7
By adopting simple risk analysis, a judgment can be made whether contingency
sums should be budgeted in the scheme in order to cope with occurrences of this
nature that are likely and where their impact may threaten the project.
Client resources
The client’s knowledge, the experience of the client company’s organisation and the
environment in which it operates are vital in assessing the appropriate procure-
ment strategy. Project objectives are influenced by the nature and culture of the
client organisation, external influences and the expectations of individuals involved
in or affected by the project. The extent to which the client is prepared to take a full
and active role is a major consideration.
Project characteristics
The size, complexity and location of the project should be carefully considered and
particular attention given to projects with novel elements. For example, if the build-
ing is especially large or complex there may be a bigger risk of cost or time overrun.
Novel projects present special risks. The novelty factor potentially means that esti-
mates of time, cost and performance are all subject to the possibility of greater error
with an increased risk of failing to achieve one or more of the project’s objectives.
Ability to make changes
It is preferable to identify the total needs of the project during the early stages but
this is not always possible. Rapidly changing technology often means late changes.
Changes in the scope of the project very often result in increased costs, especially if
they arise during construction. Changes introduced after the design is well
advanced or construction has commenced often have a disproportionate effect on
the project, in terms of cost, delay and disruption, compared with the change itself.
The design process goes through a progressive series of ‘freezes’ as it develops, but
the client or project team should set a final design freeze date after which no
significant changes to requirements or design are allowed.
Some procurement strategies are better than others at handling the introduction
of changes later in the project and reducing the possibility of having to pay some
form of specific premium.
Risk management
The choice of procurement route will affect the extent to which a client is exposed
to risk. In some cases clients will be risk averse; other clients will be able to absorb
elements of risk in order to achieve the outcome they seek. Many clients will need
to know at the commencement of the procurement process a fairly accurate cost or
an expected completion date. Some may need to assess the outcome of the project
on their financial structure. Others will wish to ensure that the project will meet

-- 138 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	121
their functional need. The risks inherent in the various procurement routes are
outlined in this chapter and the management of risk in a broader sense is discussed
in Chapter 11.
Cost issues
Estimates of the cost of future buildings made at an early stage are notoriously
inaccurate but are necessary for decision-making purposes. This is an inevitable con-
sequence of the unique nature of construction projects and the typical lack of early
design development. Where there is the need for price certainty this influences both
project timing and the procurement route to be used. Generally, design should be
complete if price certainty is required before construction commences. Since design
takes time this will cause a delay unless design and build strategies are adopted.
Inflation will inevitably affect total cost where there is a level of variance, and
contracts typically include increased price clauses to compensate the contractor, to
varying degrees, for such a variance.
Changes to design during the course of construction should be avoided if cost
certainty is to be maintained. Changes, whether initiated by the client or design
team, often have cost and time implications on the project well in excess of the
change itself. It is therefore important for the client to fix a date after which no
significant changes should be introduced or to select a route that enables such
changes and accepts the consequences.
Project timing
Most projects are needed within a defined time frame or by a specific date. Timing
will influence whether subsequent activity can occur as planned and, in many
cases, may severely affect those factors identified as critical or high priority in the
business case.
Self-evidently, setting unachievable programmes will result in overruns and the
UK construction industry has a reputation for delivering projects ‘late’. Over-
optimism or lack of reliable data may be the cause. Realism may frustrate an impa-
tient client but unexpected lateness may have more severe consequences.
The project programme is influenced by many of the factors identified above
and a particularly large or complex project is likely to require more time for design,
specification and construction than would a simple small building.
It is vitally important to ensure adequate design time for the total project, par-
ticularly if design is required to be complete before construction commences
(where perhaps cost certainty is required). Design can be a complex and lengthy
process, often taking as long prior to the commencement of the works as the works
do themselves.
In the process of the appointment of the design team, assurances should be
obtained about resource levels and the ability to meet key dates or programmes. It
is not usual to impose contractual dates upon designers, although their progress is
probably key to the overall completion date.

-- 139 of 346 --

122 	Chapter 7
Decisions to progress with a project may be influenced planning control and
approval policies, by the successful operation of compulsory purchase orders, by
land purchase or by some other non-specific but critical factor (such as obtaining
funding approval). Depending on whether these factors occur earlier or later, they
may be an influence upon the planned or desired time available for design.
Some procurement routes enable an overlap between the design and construc-
tion stages, such that construction can start earlier than would be the case with
more sequential strategies, and therefore offer the potential for earlier completion.
It may be necessary to review the planned route in the light of design progress at
the point where restraints to construction are removed, bearing in mind the stage
of design and the consequence in terms of risk.
Time, for the construction client, has both a cost and a value. If the worth of a
project is identifiable then the cost of relatively late completion and the value of
relatively early completion can be assessed and may form an important factor in
the decision-making process. This is often referred to as a ‘time/cost trade-off ’. In
the case of commercial projects, achieving early completion may bring significant
value benefits including improved cash flows and reduced interest and insurance
charges. On the other hand, later completion will render the client’s cash flow
position less attractive, will attract higher interest and insurance charges (amongst
others) and may lead to potential loss of opportunity. Developing calculations that
identify these sums can be a useful management tool.
Construction times
Total construction time is a consequence of design. More complex structures will
almost certainly take longer given the same cost or size, and may require more
resources. Although it is possible to accelerate work on site, for example by working
longer hours or by increasing resources, it is not always possible to achieve directly
proportional productivity gains. The law of diminishing returns will have an influ-
ence because of the limited space and the nature of traditional construction meth-
ods (such as concreting and bricklaying).
Performance
The required performance of the project, measured in terms both of its response to
the needs of the client as expressed in the business case and of the quality of indi-
vidual elements, should be clearly identified. If performance is over-specified, a
premium will be paid for exceeding actual requirements, thereby affecting the cost
objective. Over-specification may also lead to time overruns. Conversely, failure to
recognise the true performance objectives leads to an unsatisfactory product.
If quality and performance are particularly important the client will probably
want to keep direct control over the development of the design. This can be
achieved by employing the design team directly, or by developing carefully written
and precise performance specifications, supported by performance measures, for
both the completed building and its principal functional components.

-- 140 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	123
Selection of a procurement route
Clients who can be described as experienced will be able to select a procurement
approach that has worked for them before or which they know to be suitable, tak-
ing into account their prioritised objectives and their attitude to risk. Inexperienced
clients (the majority of clients in the UK fall into this category) will need to seek
advice from experienced professionals to help them through the process.
It is most important that the adopted route is reviewed at key stages during the
progress of the project, for example the planning approval stage, before the con-
tract strategy has been finally decided, and before the construction contracts are
let. Reasons for this include that the development of design is not maintaining the
pace anticipated or that the programme is otherwise affected by unexpected
occurrences and that, consequently, an alternative route may become more
appropriate.
The selection of an appropriate procurement route has two components:
(1) 	Analysis: assessing and setting the priorities of the project objectives and
client attitude to risk.
(2) 	Choice: considering possible options, evaluating them and selecting the most
appropriate.
It may be necessary to seek specialist advice from other consultants, for example,
in relation to expected costs for the project. Specialist advice should always be
sought when developing the strategy for novel or especially difficult projects.
In 1996, RICS published The Procurement Guide, which was then updated as
part of the Surveyors’ Construction Handbook (RICS 1996, 2000). RICS publish fur-
ther updated guidance on procurement, which includes an extensive range of
advice and information for chartered surveyors engaged in construction activity.
These guides are intended to provide a code of procedure to help in developing an
appropriate strategy and selecting the right procurement route for a building pro-
ject. The guides are intended to be used as a prompt and focus for the issues to be
addressed during the development of the procurement strategy, while setting the
standards to be achieved by chartered surveyors when advising their clients.
It is however important to note that whilst such guidance provides an overview
of good procurement practice, it is not a substitute for experience and, at least for
more complex projects, clients would be well advised to seek out advisors with
extensive procurement experience and proven expertise. The selected procure-
ment route must be developed from an objective assessment of the client’s needs
and project characteristics. The objective is to provide the client with advice, thus
enabling an informed client to reach an appropriate decision based on sound judg-
ment, giving due regard to the identified criteria and the acceptable distribution of
risk. Figure 7.1 shows a checklist for comparing the various strengths and weak-
nesses of different procurement systems.
One approach to objective selection is to develop the information provided by
the Priority Distribution Analysis (see Figure 5.2) using the checklist in Figure 7.1.

126 	Chapter 7
Procurement routes: a review
Hughes (2004) has argued that since there are so many variables to each of the
commonly adopted procurement routes, notwithstanding the commonly adopted
nomenclature, there is a very wide range of routes available. This may be so, but
there are a range of commonly adopted basic procurement frameworks, each of
which is discussed below.
Traditional (design–bid–build)
This is probably still the most commonly adopted UK route, particularly for
inexperienced or occasional construction clients. The design is completed before
the selection of a contractor to build the works. It is seen as the least risk approach
for the client as there is an inherent level of certainty about design, cost and dura-
tion if it is properly implemented. The sequential nature does mean that it can be
relatively slow prior to the commencement of construction.
Under a traditional procurement route, design should be completed before
competitive tenders are invited and before the main construction contract is let. As
a result, and assuming no changes are introduced, construction costs can be
determined with reasonable certainty before construction starts. This may be
particularly attractive to clients with a strictly limited budget or a limit to their
borrowing powers.
The tender documents include drawn designs and a specification of workman-
ship and materials which the contractor must use in the preparation of a price. In
some cases the client will arrange to appoint a quantity surveyor to measure the
quantity of work to be done in order to satisfy the demands of the design. In such
cases each contractor will submit a price upon the same work extent and the client
will be responsible for the accuracy of these quantities prepared in the form of a bill
Table 7.3 	Investor client: typical values transferred from Figure 5.2.
Design
and Build 	Traditional

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	127
of quantities. Whilst such documents reduce the contractors’ tendering costs,
significantly fewer of them have been used in the last 25 years, shifting the risk for
assessing the extent of the work back to the contractor.
The tender documents supplied to the contractors bidding for the work may
include drawings and a specification without quantities. Consequently the costs of
the bidding process are increased as all tenderers will have to carry out some form
of quantification for which they have to take the risk of error.
The contractor assumes responsibility and risk for the construction of the
building works for the agreed contract sum, to the design produced (usually) by the
client’s architect, and within the contract period, whilst the client takes the respon-
sibility and risk for the design and design team performance. Therefore, if the
contractor’s works are delayed by the failure of the design team to meet their obliga-
tions, the contractor may seek recompense from the client for any additional costs
and time to complete the project. In turn, the client could seek to recover these
costs from the design team members responsible, if negligence could be proved.
Clients are able to influence the development of the design to meet their require-
ments because they have direct contractual relationships with the design team.
When construction begins, they usually have a single contractual relationship with
a main contractor, but are usually only able to influence (not control) the construc-
tion process through their architect, acting as their agent for this purpose.
The strategy may fail to some degree if any attempt is made to appoint a contrac-
tor for the work before the design is complete. Such action will probably result in
many post-contract changes which could delay the progress of the works and
increase the costs. Where a traditional route is chosen because of its particular
advantages, the temptation to let the work before the design is complete should be
strongly resisted.
It is possible to accelerate the pace of the traditional procurement route by
adopting a two-stage tendering approach where the contractor is selected on the
basis of prices submitted on an approximate or notional bill of quantities. The final
contract sum will be negotiated as the design is completed. This has the potentially
significant advantage of enabling the contractor to contribute to the development
of the design, but the extent to which this might be useful will usually depend upon
the type of the project and the completeness of the design at the time the contrac-
tor is appointed. Also, depending upon the nature and degree of accuracy of the
initial approximate quantities, particularly perhaps in terms of specification, ine-
quality in the relative strengths of the parties’ negotiating positions may leave the
client paying a higher price than would have been the case had the work been
tendered on completion of the design. In general the more novel and complex the
design, the more likely it is that the contractor will be able to contribute ideas
which will improve constructability and may thus lead to savings in construction
time and, often to a lesser extent, construction cost. Inequalities in the parties’
negotiating positions may to some extent be negated through the use of a target
cost contract where payment for the work is based upon reimbursement of the
contractor’s actual costs assessed on an open-book basis and incorporating pain
and gain share provisions, rather than on a conventional lump-sum basis. Note

-- 145 of 346 --

128 	Chapter 7
however that considerable commercial acumen will typically be required on the
part of the client and his advisors if they are to negotiate an acceptable target cost
with a contractor whose commercial interests are plainly best served by driving up
the initial target cost as high as possible. Also the assessment of ‘actual cost’ is a
highly skilled process demanding considerable knowledge of contractors’ costing
systems and accounting procedures. This task is, in truth, much more akin to
forensic accountancy than the cost management skills normally practiced by a
consultant quantity surveyor.
Some time-saving can usually be achieved by letting a separate, advance works
contract, for example by allowing ground works (site clearance, piling and founda-
tions) to proceed to construction once planning permission has been obtained and
before the design for the rest of the building is completed, with the above-ground
construction being tendered separately at a later date. This generally reduces the
total time to complete the project, but at the risk of losing certainty of cost before
construction starts. More importantly, a substantial risk is created in that the con-
tractor who builds the superstructure has no responsibility for the foundation
works carried out by another contractor, and this may pose major difficulties in
the event that warranties are required by funders, tenants or subsequent third
party purchasers of the completed building. In each of these cases the certainty of
end cost is reduced in the search for a faster start but the other risks may still be
contained.
The organisational structure of a traditional route is shown in Figure 7.2. A wide
range of standard forms of contract are available, enabling a common understand-
ing of rights and responsibilities, and these forms are usually updated to take
account of, for example, changes in the law.

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	129
The main advantages of a traditional contract route are as follows:
■	■ 	Competitive fairness, since all tendering contractors are bidding on the same
basis.
■	■ 	Design-led, with the client able to have direct influence, thus facilitating a high
level of functionality and bespoke quality in the design.
■	■ 	Reasonable price certainty at contract award based upon market forces (subject
always to design changes or client-led changes which will have cost implications).
■	■ 	Where public expenditure or audit demands are rigid the route is satisfactory in
terms of public accountability since it is transparent and based upon competition.
■	■ 	The procedures are well known, enabling confidence to be assured in those
involved throughout the supply chain.
■	■ 	Changes are reasonably easy to arrange and to value where the design needs
vary due to changes in client need or technology (though this ease of change
can prove a disadvantage).
The main disadvantages are that:
■	■ 	It is possible to attempt to speed up the process by producing tender documents
from an incomplete design, but this will usually result in less cost and time
certainty and can be the cause of expensive disputes.
■	■ 	The overall project duration may be longer than for other routes as the strategy
is sequential and construction cannot be commenced prior to the completion
of design with no parallel working.
■	■ 	There is no input into the design or planning of the project by the contractor,
who will not be appointed at the design stage.
■	■ 	The route is based upon price competition and this can result in adversarial
relationships.
Design and build
The use of this approach has become increasingly popular over the last 25 years
(RICS 2010). Under a design and build route, a single contractor assumes the risk
and responsibility for designing and building the project, usually in return for a
fixed-price lump sum. Design and build is usually regarded as a fast-track route, in
that construction can start before all the detailed design is completed, with the
contractor completing the design as the work progresses. There is a broad range of
variants on the design and build arrangement and researchers have disagreed on
how they should be categorised. For example, Andrews (1999) identified five
variants and Akintoye (1994) identified six. Gidado and Arshi (2004) propose the
following typology:
(A) 	Traditional design and build: The contractor accepts the total responsibility
for design and construction to meet the requirement of the client.
(B) 	Package deal (including turnkey contracts): The contractor provides stand-
ard buildings or system buildings that are adapted to suit the client’s space
and functional requirements.

-- 147 of 346 --

130 	Chapter 7
(C) 	Design and manage: The contractor gets a fee for managing all aspects of
planning and design and supervising the subcontractors. The contractor has
design responsibility.
(D) 	Design, manage and construct: This is similar to ‘design and manage’ except
that the contractor is involved in construction of some work sections in addi-
tion to coordinating the activities of the subcontractors on site.
(E) 	Novation design and build: The client passes his architect to the contractor
to produce detailed drawings as part of the contractor’s team. During the
design stage through to the appointment of the design and build contractor,
the architect works directly for and is paid by the client. Once a contractor
has been appointed, the architect’s appointment is assigned to the contractor
for whom the architect produces any outstanding information necessary to
construct the work. In some cases, once practical completion of the work has
been reached, the architect reverts back to being employed by the client.
Beyond this stage, the architect prepares the list of defective and outstanding
items, monitors their completion and certifies the completion of the project
at the end of the defects liability period.
(F) 	Develop and construct: This is a hybrid process in which the contractor inher-
its a design that might have been produced by the client’s consultants up to
Stage D on the RIBA Architect’s Appointment Scale. This is developed further
by the contractor, taking into account the construction technique to be adopted
for the project. This is different from novation in the sense that the architect
who provides the concept design is not passed to the contractor by the client.
There is a reduction in risk for the client in contracting with a single organisa-
tion for both design and construction in that, unlike the traditional approach
where design is carried out by one organisation and construction by another, there
is no possibility of divided responsibility for defects in the completed works. There
is however an increased risk that control over the design and specification may be
substantially lost. It is very important for the client to reduce this risk, firstly by
taking great care in selecting the contractor to carry out the work and secondly by
clearly specifying the requirements and constraints of the project, generally in the
form of detailed performance specifications against which the actual performance
of the completed building and its constituent elements and systems can be objec-
tively measured. The document setting out these requirements is often termed the
Statement of Employer’s Requirements, and it is for the tendering contractors to
put forward Contractor’s Proposals which address the expressed requirements.
Note however that any client requirement that is not directly specified in the
Statement of Employer’s Requirements provided to those contractors tendering for
the work will constitute a variation to the contract. Assessment of the price to be
paid for variations under a design and build contract is frequently difficult, and the
cost of such changes to the client will generally be greater than would be the case
under a traditionally procured project with a Bill of Quantities.
Note however that, under most standard forms of contract, the bargain is formed
by the client’s acceptance of the Contractor’s Proposals and it is therefore the client’s

-- 148 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	131
responsibility to ensure that such proposals adequately address the Employer’s
Requirements. It should also be noted that, at common law, design and build con-
tractors have a ‘fitness for purpose’ liability (see for example Viking Grain Storage v
T.H. White Installations (1985)1 and Independent Broadcasting Authority v EMI
Electronics and BICC Construction Ltd (1978)2) rather than the less onerous ‘due
skill and care’ obligation of a consultant designer (Bolam v Friern Hospital
Management Committee (1957) 3 ). Design and build contractors may however
exclude their common law obligation and replace it with the obligation to use ‘due
skill and care’. This approach is supported by a number of widely used standard
forms of contract although some, notably the NEC3 form (NEC 2005), require
‘fitness for purpose’ as standard with the option to reduce to ‘due skill and care’
provided as an alternative which clients must actively choose. In recent years it has
become more common for employers to seek to impose a ‘fitness for purpose’ obli-
gation on design and build contractors, but such obligations may be difficult to
enforce in their entirety unless the meaning of ‘fitness for purpose’ is clearly defined
in the contract. Some have attempted to do this by requiring their Statement of
Employer’s Requirements to be a contract document taking precedence over the
Contractor’s Proposals. Note however that the issue of fitness for purpose is a very
thorny one. Githunguri (2012) provides a useful summary of the legal position.
The imposition on contractors of fitness for purpose in design is a matter of
judgement for clients and their professional advisers, even though tenderers in
recessionary markets are unlikely to refuse to undertake such risks. The require-
ment for insurance to cover a higher than normal risk should be weighed against
the financial ability of contractors to meet design default claims. It may represent
better value for money to impose a lesser, yet insurable, liability, which will be the
subject of an insurance payout in the event of a design fault, rather than a fitness for
purpose requirement on a contractor with limited financial assets.
Variants E and F of the typology given earlier (Gidado and Arshi 2004) are them-
selves variations of a strategy generically termed ‘develop and construct’, under
which the client appoints designers to prepare the concept design, perhaps but not
always as far as RIBA Stage D, before the contractor assumes responsibility for
completing the detailed design and constructing the works. By transferring design
risk to the contractor, the client is able to ensure that the initial design and specifi-
cation meets his needs but, as with any design and build variant, loses some control
over the latter stages of project design.
Under this approach, the client will usually employ a design team to carry out
some preliminary design, to prepare the project brief and other tender documents,
and to assist him with the preparation of the Statement of Employer’s Requirements.
Sometimes, the successful contractor will voluntarily assume responsibility for this
design team and use it to produce the detailed design. Where this is the case, con-
tracts usually require the contractor to ‘complete the design for the works’; until
1 33 BLR.
2 14 BLR 1.
3 1 WLR 582; [1957] 2 All ER 118; [1955–95] PNLR 7, QBD.

-- 149 of 346 --

132 	Chapter 7
2002, it was generally considered that the contractor took no liability for the
adequacy of any design work carried out by the consultant. It was however found,
in Cooperative Insurance Society Ltd v Henry Boot Scotland (2002) 4 that the contrac-
tor’s obligations in completing the design included a responsibility to assess and
verify the assumptions upon which the original design was based.
A second option (variant E of the typology given earlier) provides for the
contractor to agree to novate the contract the client has with the designers to the
contractor. Under a novation arrangement the contractor effectively ‘steps into the
client’s shoes’ and takes over the client’s responsibilities in respect of the consult-
ant’s existing contract. Designers do, however, sometimes resist being novated to
contractors. If a design and build route is identified as a possibility at an early stage,
then the basis of the appointment of the design team should reflect the possibility
of novation. If it does not, the client may have to pay a termination fee to the design
team. The client may wish to retain the independent services of a cost consultant
throughout the contract for early cost advice involvement in the bidding process
and cost reporting during construction.
Consideration may need to be given to the inclusion of a special clause in design,
or design and build, contracts to ensure that the responsibility for design perfor-
mance is properly allocated or shared. Such a clause should identify specific obliga-
tions that are absolute, that do not require the designer to be an expert in the client’s
business and, as a consequence, are reasonably insurable.
Increasingly, in the wake of cases such as Darlington Borough Council v Wiltshire
Northern Ltd (1995)5 and the lengthy saga of Alfred McAlpine Construction v
Panatown Ltd (2001),6 collateral warranties are being demanded by funders, ten-
ants and third party purchasers to place additional responsibility on designers or
subcontractors. Such problems may however now be dealt with in the UK under the
contract using the provisions of the Contracts (Rights of Third Parties) Act 1999.
Clients may also choose to take out latent defects insurance to cover post-
construction liability, usually covering defects due to faulty design, workmanship
or materials. The policy is usually for 10–12 years for a single premium and is
assignable, but this will be at a relatively high cost. This is a matter for specialist
advice from insurance experts.
In short, design and build provides a range of options from a package deal or
turnkey solution, in which the client has little involvement in the design develop-
ment or building procurement process (effectively, a complete hands-off approach),
to a develop and construct solution, in which the client appoints designers to
develop his or her brief to a level of sophistication which will leave the design and
build contractor to develop detailed or specialist design elements.
The generic project organisation structure for design and build is shown in
Figure 7.3. Contractors may use their own firm’s resources for undertaking the
design (in-house design and build) or, more likely, may outsource them to one or
4 EWHC 1270 (TCC).
5 1 WLR 68; [1995] 3 All ER 895; [1994] CLC 691, CA (Civ Div).
6 Unreported, 27 July 2000, HL.

-- 150 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	133
more consultancy firms, whilst retaining control. Standard forms of design and build
contract are available.
The main advantages of a design and build route are as follows:
■	■ 	The client has to deal with only one firm, significantly reducing the need to com-
mit resources and time to contracting with designers and contractors separately.
■	■ 	The approach enables the integration of the constructor and specialist subcon-
tractors to contribute to the design and project planning.
■	■ 	Price certainty is obtained before construction starts provided the client’s
requirements are adequately specified and changes are not introduced.
■	■ 	It is possible to reduce the total project time due to early completion because of
overlapping activities.
The disadvantages are that:
■	■ 	Difficulties can be experienced by clients in preparing an adequate and
sufficiently comprehensive brief and in defining what they require from the
contractor’s proposals in order to assess them at bid stage.
■	■ 	The client is required to commit to a concept design at an early stage and often
before the detailed designs are completed.
■	■ 	Bids are difficult to compare since each design will be different, the project
programme will vary between bidders and prices for the project will be differ-
ent for each different design.
■	■ 	There is no design overview unless separate consultants are appointed by the
client for this purpose. Quality may be compromised as the client has relin-
quished control to the design and build contractor.
■	■ 	Client changes to project scope can be expensive.
■	■ 	Design liability is often limited by the standard contracts available.

Employer’s agent	Advisors
Figure 7.3 	Organisational structure for a construction management for a design and build approach.

-- 151 of 346 --

134 	Chapter 7
Measurement (remeasurement or measure and value)
This procurement route is only occasionally adopted for building works but is
commonly used for civil engineering projects where there is a level of uncertainty
in terms of the ground conditions. The organisational structure of the route is
identical to the traditional route (see Figure 7.2) but the contract sum is only
established with certainty on completion of construction, when remeasurement of
the quantities of work actually carried out takes place. The basis for the final sum
to be paid is the tender prices provided by the contractor on the basis of a bill of
approximate quantities or even a schedule of rates.
Measurement contracts are sometimes referred to as ‘remeasurement’ or ‘meas-
ure and value’ contracts and are based upon the principle that the work carried
out is measured and valued at the prices for each type of work tendered by the
contractor.
The most effective use of a measurement contract is where the work has been
substantially designed but the final detail has not been completed. Here, as with
civil engineering projects, a tender based on drawings and a bill of approximate
quantities will be satisfactory.
Measurement contracts allow a client to shorten the overall programme for
design, tendering and construction but usually with some lack of early price cer-
tainty because the approximate quantities reflect the lack of information on exactly
what is to be built at tender stage. The scope of the work, the approximate price and
a programme should be clear at the contract stage. Measurement contracts provide
more risk for the client than the sort of lump sum contracts achieved through
design–bid–build contracts but probably with programme advantages.
The organisational structure of a measurement contract route is identical to the
traditional approach except that it is not a lump sum contract. Standard forms of
contract are available.
The main advantages of a measurement contract include the following:
■	■ 	Potential for pre-construction time-saving, as the later aspects of the design are
still ongoing as the works are progressing on site.
■	■ 	Competitive prices, as the work is tendered on standard approximate quantities
which will be used to value the completed work on site.
■	■ 	Some public accountability where this is required due to the competitive selec-
tion process.
■	■ 	Well-known procedures, particularly in civil engineering projects.
■	■ 	Changes can be made easily as the later stages of the design are still progressing.
■	■ 	Parallel working possible, as the contractor is selected before the design and
project planning processes are completed.
The main disadvantages are that:
■	■ 	The route offers poor certainty of price since the cost to the client will not be
accurately known to the client until the works are complete. This will depend
upon the level of certainty in the approximate quantities used in the tendering
process.

-- 152 of 346 --

Procurement strategies and procurement routes 	135
■	■ 	Programme length will be affected if the pace of design is not maintained.
■	■ 	There is no contractor involvement in the early planning or design stage when
most of the expensive or time-impacting decisions are made.
■	■ 	There is potential for adversarial relationships to develop, as with all strategies
that are price dependent. This may be more likely in this case unless a willing-
ness to negotiate is evident in both the contractor and the design team/client.
Construction management
Under a construction management route, the client does not allocate risk and
responsibility to a single main contractor. Instead, the client employs the design
team and a construction manager is engaged as a fee-earning professional to man-
age, programme and co-ordinate the design and construction activities and to
facilitate collaboration in order to improve the buildability of the design.
Construction work is carried out by trade contractors through direct contracts
with the client for distinct trade or work packages. These trade contractors
are appointed as soon as the design for that element of work is completed. This may
be at an early stage or a late stage in the construction process. Since design of the
whole project does not need to be detailed when work commences this will result
in an opportunity for a faster completion. The construction manager supervises
the construction process and co-ordinates the design team.
This route is usually adopted where the project is programme-driven. Cost
certainty is not achieved until the final trade contract is let and equally there is no
contractual completion date. The client will have to decide to progress with this
route on the basis of an estimated budget or an approximate programme.
The construction manager, who has no contractual links with the design team
or the trade contractors, provides professional construction expertise without
assuming financial risk and is liable only for negligence by failing to perform the
role with reasonable skill and care, unless some greater liability is incorporated in
the contract.
On appointment, the construction manager will take over any preliminary
scheduling and costing information already prepared and will draw up a detailed
programme of pre-construction activities. Key dates, at which client decisions will
be required, are normally inserted. In adopting the construction management sys-
tem, the client will be closely involved in each stage of design and construction.
The client must have administrative or project management staff with the time and
ability to assess the recommendations of the construction manager and take the
necessary action. The client needs to maintain a strong presence through a project
management team that is technically and commercially astute. This approach is
not, therefore, suitable for the inexpert or inexperienced client.
Better cost certainty can inevitably be achieved when as many packages as
possible are let as early as possible. This will depend upon the pace of the design
process. Also, design development of later packages can affect construction work
already completed. The construction manager should, therefore, have a good
track record in cost forecasting and cost management. A package is made up of

-- 153 of 346 --

136 	Chapter 7
work for which one of the trade contractors is responsible, e.g. foundations, con-
crete, electrical installation or decorating. These packages are tendered individu-
ally, for a lump sum price, usually on the basis of drawings and specification.
Construction management has been predominantly used in the UK for large or
complex projects, but there is no intrinsic reason for this. Indeed, it is particularly
recommended for projects where there is a high degree of design innovation, where
the client wants ‘hands on’ involvement.
The organisational structure of construction management is shown in Figure 7.4.
Standard forms of contract are available.
The main advantages of a construction management route are as follows:
■	■ 	Relative time-saving potential for overall project time due to the overlapping of
design and construction procedures.
■	■ 	The route enables constructor contribution to the design and project planning.
■	■ 	Roles, risks and relationships for all participants are clear.
■	■ 	Changes in design can be accommodated later than in some other routes, with-
out paying a premium, provided the relevant trade packages have not been let
and already awarded packages are not adversely affected.
■	■ 	The client has direct contracts with trade contractors and pays them directly.
(There is some evidence that this results in lower prices because of improved
cash flow certainty.)
The disadvantages are that:
■	■ 	Price certainty is not achieved until the last trade packages have been let.
Budgeting depends heavily upon design team estimates.

■	■ 	An informed, pro-active client is required in order to operate such a strategy.
■	■ 	The client must provide a good quality brief to the design team as the design
will not be complete until the client has committed significant resources to the
project.
■	■ 	The route relies upon the client selecting a good quality and committed team.
■	■ 	Close time and information control is required. The design team must be con-
trolled and kept on programme for the delivery of design information when
required to ensure that neither the work package procurement nor the works
on site is delayed.
Management contracting
With this approach, a management contractor is engaged by the client to manage
the whole of the building process and is paid a fee for doing so. Unlike construc-
tion management, the management contractor has direct contractual links with
all the works contractors and is responsible for all the construction works. The
management contractor, therefore, bears the responsibility for the construction
works without actually carrying out any of that work. The management contrac-
tor may provide some of the common services on site, such as office accommo-
dation, tower cranes, hoists and security, which are shared by the works
contractors, but in pure management contracting such works are let as a self-
contained work package. The client employs the design team and, therefore,
bears the risk of the design team delaying construction for reasons other than
negligence, for example late issue of design information which may cause delay
to the programme.
Management contracting is a ‘fast track’ route. All design work will not be com-
plete before the first works contractors start work, although the design necessary
for those packages must be complete unless, of course, the work contained in the
package is of a specialist nature (for example curtain walling) when packages may
be let on a design and construct basis. Consequently there is little cost certainty for
the client until construction works are well under way and it is a route usually
adopted, as with construction management, where the primary objective for the
client is relative speed to completion.
As design is completed, subsequent packages of work are tendered and let. Cost
certainty is, thus, not achieved until all works contractors have been appointed. A
high level of cost management is, therefore, required. With the agreement of the
client, the management contractor selects works contractors by competitive tender
to undertake sections of the construction work. The client reimburses the cost of
these work packages to the management contractor who, in turn, pays the works
contractors. The management contractor co-ordinates the release of information
from the design team to the works contractors.
Where the management construction team is not of the highest quality, or where
the management fee is inadequate, the management contractor can be less than
pro-active and the system can become a reactive ‘postbox’ approach. It is, therefore,
vital to select the management contractor carefully and to ensure that his or her fee

-- 155 of 346 --

138 	Chapter 7
is appropriate, bearing in mind market conditions. Similarly, resistance to works
contractors’ claims can be affected by the same circumstances.
The organisational structure of a management contract is shown in Figure 7.5.
A standard form of contract is available.
The main advantages of management contracting are as follows:
■	■ 	There are programme benefits for the overall project due to the overlapping of
the design and construction processes.
■	■ 	The constructor can contribute to the design and project planning.
■	■ 	Changes can be accommodated provided packages affected have not been let
and there is little or no impact on those already let.
■	■ 	Works packages are let competitively at prices that are current at the time the
work is let.
The disadvantages are that:
■	■ 	The client must provide a good quality brief to the design team as the design
will not be complete until the client has committed significant resources to the
project.
■	■ 	Poor certainty of price is offered at an early stage and the potential cost com-
mitment depends upon design team estimates.
■	■ 	The approach relies on a good quality committed team or it may become no
more than a ‘post box’ system in certain circumstances.
■	■ 	It reduces resistance to works contractors’ claims where such demands are
passed on to the client by the management contractor.

Design and manage
Although the design and manage approach is categorised by some researchers as a
form of design and build, the design and manage route has strong similarities to
management contracting. Under a design and manage contract, the contractor is
paid a fee to manage and assume responsibility, not only for the works contractors,
but also for the design team.
The main advantages of design and manage are as follows:
■	■ 	The client deals with one firm only, enabling improved co-ordination and
collaboration between designers and constructors.
■	■ 	There is a relatively good time-saving potential for the overall project due to the
overlapping of the design and construction processes.
■	■ 	The contractor assumes the risk and responsibility for the integration of the
design with construction.
The disadvantages are that:
■	■ 	Price certainty is not achieved until the last work package has been let.
■	■ 	The client loses direct control over the design quality which is influenced
significantly by the constructors.
■	■ 	The client has no direct contractual relationship with the works contractors or
the design team and it is, therefore, difficult for the client to recover costs if they
fail to meet their obligations.
