15 Procurement of work
to heritage buildings
Introduction
The procurement of construction work to existing buildings has always been
arguably more problematic than new-build construction and the problems tend to
multiply when the existing building is held to have some particular historical or
cultural significance. In general, the older the building the less likely it is that
records showing the detail of the building structure exist and the greater the likeli-
hood that specialist expertise, both professional and practical, will be required.
Some of this expertise, for example the historical and archaeological skills com-
monly required to properly ‘read’ and understand both the form and construction
of an existing building and the way in which it was used, will be required only very
rarely in new-build construction but are often essential where historic structures
are concerned. In addition some of the specialist craft skills, often required as a
matter of course in projects involving historic buildings, are becoming increasingly
rare and may impose major constraints on the procurement process.
Although this kind of work has always been widely believed to be important, for
example in the preservation and repair of significant religious and cultural herit-
age, it has historically formed only a comparatively minor part of overall construc-
tion activity and it has therefore been largely neglected from a procurement point
of view. It was pointed out by Davey (1992) that many, perhaps most, of the special-
ist practitioners commonly engaged in this type of work will not have had the
opportunity to work with, and become proficient in the use of, the full range of
procurement options available. It is perhaps understandable therefore that:
Many practitioners will tend to keep with the familiar and adopt a method of
procurement which they have used in the past. Although this may produce a
satisfactory contract it does not follow that it is the best solution.
There is little evidence to show that, in procurement terms at least, the situation
is any different 20 years on, but in recent years the twin desires to protect our built
heritage and to ensure greater sustainability in the built environment have sparked
a renewed interest in the rehabilitation and adaptive reuse of a wide range of

-- 295 of 346 --

278 	Chapter 15
existing built assets as an alternative to wholesale demolition and redevelopment,
in addition to the historically more common repair and maintenance work. The
breadth and scale of such adaptive reuse projects has, in the recent past, ranged
over a broad spectrum including:
■	■ 	In the housing sector, the imaginative refurbishment and adaptation of
Victorian terraced housing (Plimmer et al. (2008) present a good overview).
■	■ 	Refurbishment of schools as an alternative to demolition and redevelopment
(English Heritage (2010) give some excellent examples).
■	■ 	Major construction projects designed to display the important features of
significant heritage structures whilst at the same time giving them a new lease
of life in a totally new context by using them to provide ‘state of the art’ facilities
suited to the modern world. One fine example of a building given a new lease
of life is included as a case study at the end of this chapter.
■	■ 	Projects comprising significant programmes of maintenance and repair, exten-
sions or alterations to historic assets in order to enable them to continue to
provide their historic functions but often also with the addition of modern
facilities.
Whilst all of the principles of good procurement practice discussed elsewhere in
this book apply equally to the procurement of work to heritage buildings, they may
need to be modified to take account of the particular challenges which such pro-
jects typically present. This chapter presents an overview of these challenges and
the way in which they may be met but it must be clearly understood that what is
presented here is nothing more than a superficial review. Appropriate measures to
safeguard the care and maintenance of the historic or heritage environment will
usually require the exercise of a very specialised body of knowledge and specialist
advice and expertise should therefore be sought at an early stage.
The heritage challenge
Earl (2003) poses a series of philosophical questions which, he proposes, should be
considered by anyone concerned in any way with the care and maintenance of the
historic environment. The following issues (paraphrased and condensed) may
be particularly pertinent to the procurement of works to heritage structures and
environments:
■	■ 	What are we setting out to achieve?
■	■ 	What do we mean by historic or heritage structures and environments?
■	■ 	Why should such structures or environments be treated differently from other
structures or environments and how differently should they be treated?
■	■ 	What are our motives in altering, preserving or conserving rather than merely
maintaining and how should those motives influence actions?
■	■ 	Is every part of the old fabric so sacred that it must at all costs be preserved
whatever its state? To what extent might it be permissible to replace worn-out

-- 296 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	279
or disfigured elements and should the replacements be exact copies of the
original work or should they be clearly differentiated from it?
■	■ 	Most historic buildings and environments will have gone through a number of
phases of ageing, development, renovation, alteration and reconstruction in the
course of their life. If our purpose is to restore the building or environment to
its original condition, how is ‘original’ defined?
■	■ 	How should the practical consequences of a change of use or an environmental
change be accommodated?
■	■ 	To what extent should we expect the answers to these questions to vary with the
age, nature, degree of importance and surrounding environment of the historic
structure?
Major issues then which may require consideration in the formulation of an
appropriate procurement strategy and programme for such projects will include,
amongst others:
■	■ 	A clear understanding of the type and purpose of the work to be undertaken
(for example periodic repair and maintenance, preservation and conservation,
restoration, upgrading and refurbishment or adaptive reuse).
■	■ 	The significance of the existing asset in both historical and cultural terms.
■	■ 	The degree of statutory protection afforded to the asset.
■	■ 	Problems arising from the environment surrounding the asset, for example
projects carried out in a conservation area or within the confines of a historic
estate, which might include the need to preserve the historical, architectural or
aesthetic integrity of the asset itself and its surroundings. There may also be a
need for archaeological, historical, landscape or architectural investigations at
the project inception stage, before and during the design phase, and during the
carrying out of the work on site.
■	■ 	The likely absence of accurate drawings and other records describing the asset
and its historical environment and the consequent need for possibly extensive
and time-consuming archival study, archaeological exploratory work, measur-
ing and photographic and building condition surveys. Some of these investiga-
tions may be particularly time-consuming and expensive, for example there
may be a need for special access arrangements (such as the use of scaffolding or
hydraulic platforms) or the opening up of elements of the building structure in
order to investigate and fully understand the structure and form of the building
and its historical, cultural and sociological importance.
■	■ 	The likely need to prepare additional documentation, for example a heritage
impact assessment, conservation statement or a full conservation plan as a prel-
ude to the seeking of planning consent, conservation area consent or listed
building consent.
■	■ 	The likely need to carry out work in a number of separately defined and prob-
ably time-separated phases.
■	■ 	The possibility, and likely availability, of any grant funding for essential repairs
and conservation.

-- 297 of 346 --

280 	Chapter 15
Procurement: The heritage context
One of the major difficulties faced at an early stage by those working with historic
environments lies in the meaning of the terms used by those charged with protect-
ing the historic environment and whose permission is required before work can be
carried out to any structure which enjoys a degree of statutory protection.
Conservation, preservation, repair, replacement, reconstruction and restoration
are terms which, as Earl (2003) points out to great effect in his essay on the practice
of preservation, have historically been used interchangeably. Many construction
practitioners inexperienced in the heritage sector would find difficulty in discrim-
inating between them in a modern heritage context.
‘Conservation’ and ‘preservation’, at least in terms of their use in connection with
historic buildings, are to some extent synonymous and are generally taken to mean
the process of arresting the deterioration of historic fabric. The guiding principle of
conservation may be summarised as being ‘based on a respect for the existing
fabric’(ICOMOS 1999). Intervention should not distort the evidence provided by
the fabric. The remaining terms are rather more problematic. Repair, replacement,
reconstruction and restoration are not in any sense synonymous.
‘Repair’ implies restoring the functionality of something which has become
defective, often by the addition of additional components. Repair of a damaged or
decayed structural timber might, for example, comprise cutting out the decayed
section and bolting on or piecing in new pieces of timber, perhaps with steel rein-
forcing plates. English Heritage (Brereton 1995) defines the primary purpose of
repair in the context of historic buildings as:
to restrain the process of decay without damaging the character of buildings
and monuments, altering the features which give them their historic or archi-
tectural importance, or unnecessarily disturbing or destroying historic fabric.
‘Replacement’, however, would imply complete removal of the existing damaged
timber and insertion of a new timber or, perhaps, a steel section, with no attempt
being made to make the work look like anything other than a modern intervention.
‘Reconstruction’ is usually taken as meaning the process of constructing a modern
copy of the historic original.
The modern dictionary definition of ‘restoration’ in the context of historic arte-
facts, is ‘the process of restoring a building, work of art, etc. to its original condition’
(Oxford Dictionary 2011). In effect, it returns the historic fabric to the way it was
before it decayed or became damaged. Such a process is plainly problematic; any
attempt at ‘restoration’, even if we were to use traditional materials and techniques,
would plainly not return the asset to its original condition, since by definition the
new work would not be ‘original’. This might appear to be a somewhat pedantic point,
but it is, nonetheless, frequently hotly argued among the heritage community. Current
authoritative opinions range from that expressed by the Society for the Protection of
Ancient Buildings, ‘only as a practical expedient on a small scale can a case for resto-
ration be argued’ (SPAB 2009a), to the English Heritage (2007) view that:

-- 298 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	281
Restoration is an intervention made with the deliberate intention of revealing
or recovering some element of heritage value that has been eroded, obscured
or previously been removed, rather than simply maintaining the status quo.
It may also achieve other conservation benefits, for example restoring a roof
on a roofless building may make it physically and economically sustainable
in the long term.
Even if we accept the English Heritage view, we are faced with a set of further
questions, for example:
■	■ 	If we were to attempt to ‘restore’ a building or a landscape to a state at which it
existed at some time in the past, which particular point in its history would we
choose and why?
■	■ 	Do we have the evidence to be able to reproduce accurately the form, appear-
ance and methods of construction of the missing elements? Worthing and
Bond (2008) express the mainstream conservation view when they write that
‘restoration of lost features may be justified so long as there is no loss of historic
fabric and that sufficient evidence exists for accurate replacement’ but the
Venice Charter (ICOMOS 1964) makes it clear that it is equally important that
any modern intervention must be clearly distinguishable from the original
fabric ‘so that restoration does not falsify the artistic or historic evidence’.
■	■ 	Are we taking something away from the character and value of what remains by
turning an honest (even if heavily repaired, decayed or decrepit) historical
artefact into merely a modern copy? A good example might be the ‘restoration’
of a neglected historic building which, in its unrestored, possibly even ruined,
form constitutes an important and valued element in a widely admired and
important picturesque landscape.
The practical application of these principles is illustrated in the case study at the
end of this chapter.
It should also be noted that the basic philosophy underpinning the way historic
built assets have typically been cared for has been perceived differently in different
regions of the world and by different cultures. For example in Chinese conserva-
tion thinking, buildings tend to be viewed very much as repositories of ideas and
collective memory, as reminders of historical events; whilst age is venerated, it is
also quite logical to suggest that preserving such buildings in their original,
possibly decayed, form might not be necessarily desirable. Modification of such
structures at various times, often with the expressed objective of arresting decay
but also sometimes with the aim of improved utility, is therefore regarded as per-
fectly legitimate in maintaining the building ‘fit for purpose’ (D’Ayala and Wang
2006). Such perceptions were once also current in the western world, but have
shifted over time such that much of the work done with the best of intentions dur-
ing the nineteenth century by some of the most eminent architects of the time is
now regarded as nothing less than cultural vandalism. Earl (2003) points out that,
in many cases of historical restoration, ‘authenticity has not always been an over-
riding consideration’.

-- 299 of 346 --

282 	Chapter 15
Eugène Emmanuel Viollet-le-Duc, an eminent French architect, archaeologist
and mediaeval scholar, believed that restoration was essentially about ‘re-establishing
a state of completion which may never have existed at any given moment in the
past’ (Earl 2003). His exercise of this philosophy in the reconstruction of Sainte-
Chapelle in Paris involved him in demolishing substantial sections of the original
mediaeval building and replacing them with his own ‘improved’ design.
The cathedral ‘restorations’ by eminent nineteenth-century architects such as
George Nicholson, James Wyatt and Sir George Gilbert Scott were heavily criticised
at the time by John Ruskin, William Morris and several of their Gothic revival
architect peers (for example Augustus Welby Pugin) for their ‘destruction’ of work
originally carried out by, among others, Sir Christopher Wren. Nicholson for exam-
ple allegedly re-faced Durham Cathedral by chopping between two and three
inches of stone from the outer face and Wyatt compounded the sin by putting for-
ward plans to demolish the Norman Chapter House with a view to ‘improving’ the
existing building.
Earl (2003), in a very pertinent observation of the way perceptions of value
change over time as societies develop, also makes the point that these men did not
perceive what they were doing as anything which would in any way diminish the
‘value’ of the buildings in their care. Rather, they saw themselves as preserving the
value of the building by averting total loss. He concedes that, in many cases, their
interventions created unique townscapes, particularly in respect of the settings of
buildings such as historic cathedrals, which are now seen as an ‘admirable norm, to
be preserved in unspoilt completeness’.
Views similar to those of the nineteenth-century architects were once commonly
put forward in the name of art. Some commentators have proposed that important
historical remains might legitimately be ‘improved’ to make them artistically more
desirable. Pilcher (1947) for example reported that the Reverend William Gilpin,
termed by some the ‘High Priest of the Picturesque movement’, went so far as to sug-
gest, in his 1782 Observations on the River Wye, that ‘a mallet judiciously used’ might
render the, in his view insufficiently, ruined gables of Tintern Abbey more attractive.
The modern philosophy underpinning any intervention in heritage buildings
has its roots in the Manifesto published in 1877 by the Society for the Protection of
Ancient Buildings. The Manifesto (SPAB 2009) established the following four key
‘minimum intervention’ principles, which still effectively form the bedrock of
Western heritage management philosophy:
■	■ 	The present generation is the custodian of the ancient buildings they have
inherited and they should act accordingly.
■	■ 	Effective and honest repair should always be the first consideration.
■	■ 	Conservation should do no more than is necessary to look after the building.
■	■ 	Any permanent new work should be clearly distinguishable from the old and
should not reproduce past style.
In 1931 the Athens Conference organised by the International Museums Office
established the first attempt at an international code of conservation practice; it was
subsequently developed into the International Charter for the Conservation of

-- 300 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	283
Monuments and Sites (ICOMOS) by the Congress of Architects and Specialists of
Historic Buildings at a meeting in Venice in 1964. The Venice Charter (ICOMOS
1964) became the defining international standard. Subsequent meetings of
ICOMOS have published a number of amendments and additions which have had
the effect of customising the Venice Charter to take account of local circumstances.
Arguably among the most significant of these are the Burra Charter (ICOMOS
1999) and the Xi’an Declaration on the Conservation of the Setting of Heritage
Structures, Sites and Areas (ICOMOS 2005). The Burra Charter was initially agreed
in Australia in 1979 and has been modified on several occasions since. It intro-
duced the concept of cultural significance in addition to architectural and histori-
cal significance.
Notwithstanding the above, it is clear that it is not practically possible to preserve
in its original state everything which is old or interesting. Sir Neil Cossons, Pro-
Provost and Chairman of the Council of the Royal College of Art and Chairman of
English Heritage from 2000 to 2007, points out that (Clark 2001, preface):
Caring for this historic environment is a dynamic process which involves
managing change in order to allow future generations to understand what we
value and something of their origins. This does not mean keeping everything
from the past but it does involve making careful judgements about value and
significance. . . . Crucial to these decisions is an understanding of what
matters and why.
Many historic structures, for example much of the eighteenth and nineteenth cen-
tury industrial heritage and, arguably in the UK, some of the great country houses,
have outlived their initial purpose. If they are to survive at all, they must change to
accommodate new uses. The problem of course is that as soon as we change the
purpose or function of a structure we lose something of the original. We cannot, for
example convert an eighteenth century country house into a modern hotel or a nine-
teenth century cotton mill into residential units or a shopping mall without making
compromises. Frequently, it is the extent to which the architectural, historical or
cultural significance of the building or its surrounding environment is compromised
which is controversial and which has given rise to conflicts between people keen to
maximise the profitability of their asset and heritage conservers equally keen to pre-
serve the integrity of the existing building. Nonetheless the concept of adaptive reuse
of historic structures to give them a new lease of life is seen as a fundamentally
important element in heritage preservation. Russell, Smith and Leverton (2010)
present a good overview of the arguments and some excellent examples.
At the root of any successful intervention in the historic built environment lies a
clear understanding of the nature and significance of the building and its sur-
roundings; an issue explored in detail by Clark (2001). She adopts a structured
methodology termed Conservation Based Research and Analysis (CoBRA), which
she explicitly defines as:
The research, analysis, survey and investigation necessary to understand the
significance of a building and its landscape, and thus inform decisions about
repair, alteration, use and management.

-- 301 of 346 --

284 	Chapter 15
We therefore need to explore how we might define the significance (the ‘value’)
of heritage buildings and their surroundings and how the assessment of such
significance might impact upon the construction procurement process.
The value of built heritage
Professor Randall Mason, a respected academic at the University of Pennsylvania
and heritage expert, in the course of a debate at a conference exploring the public
value of heritage, is reported to have said (Clark 2006):
In the US you have to prove that heritage pays; that to protect something will
be more profitable than neglecting it. But you also have to realise that, in the
case of the heritage vs Walmart, the heritage will never win.
Although Mason’s comments were no doubt intended in a light-hearted way, they
nonetheless neatly summarise the perennial divide between heritage conservers
and commercial interests.
On the one hand conservationists fight to protect and preserve what they
perceive to be priceless historic assets which, they contend, often serve both to
preserve important elements of local or national history and culture and to provide
a community focus through the preservation of what has come to be termed ‘col-
lective memory’. On the other hand developers and property owners seek to max-
imise the commercial worth of their investment. This debate may have very
significant impacts upon any associated procurement process. It is also worthy of
note that the balance which has historically been struck between these potentially
opposing views has tended to shift over time as society’s values ebb and flow; com-
pare for example the present vogue for the adaptive reuse of historic buildings with
the wholesale demolition resulting from the ‘brave new world’ approach espoused
by the town-centre developers of the 1970s.
Controversial strategic planning and development issues continue to bring the
debate into sharp focus; consider the development of the proposed high-speed rail
link (HS2) between London and Birmingham, which is said to require the demoli-
tion of a large number of historic buildings, and the once abandoned (but now
perhaps to be resurrected) scheme for a projected sixth terminal at London’s
Heathrow Airport which was reported (Beattie 2007) to require the demolition of
‘the whole village of Sipson – and 102 listed buildings’.
If Mason’s comments were to be held to be universally true, then the only value
system which matters would be a financial one and all other notions of value, such
as historic importance, cultural significance and aesthetic merit, would be immedi-
ately nullified. This is surely an oversimplification: in many countries, the historic
built environment receives a degree of protection, usually through some form of
listing or scheduling process and through the planning system, although the degree
of protection provided is of course subject to politicians accepting a balanced view
of the competing demands of the various special interest groups involved. In the
UK for example, in early 2012, the extent of the protection offered to heritage assets

-- 302 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	285
through the planning system is considered by many to be at serious risk as a result
of government plans to ‘rebalance’ the planning system in favour of ‘sustainable
development’.
Such decisions should not to be based solely upon financial considerations, but
ought to take into account a fair and balanced reflection of all of the various dimen-
sions of value; the question which then arises is how these alternative dimensions
are to be defined. Smith (2010) contends that the following questions are important
and presents a review of how these additional dimensions might be measured:
■	■ 	What aspects of our built heritage should be regarded as ‘valuable’ and why?
■	■ 	What ‘value’ does the built heritage add to society?
■	■ 	How should we measure how ‘valuable’ built heritage is both in absolute terms
and in relative terms compared to alternatives such as redevelopment?
■	■ 	How should such ‘values’ be defined?
Consideration of these alternative dimensions of value, whether in economic,
social, cultural or environmental terms, then becomes central to modern notions
of how that heritage should be managed, particularly in terms of which elements of
our built heritage should be preserved, conserved, restored, maintained, relocated,
ignored or demolished or, controversially, whether some of the essence of the built
form could be preserved or made available in other ways, thus enabling scarce eco-
nomic resources, especially land, to be made available for redevelopment.
Tessa Jowell, a former UK Secretary of State for Culture Media and Sport, for
example was bold enough to suggest that digital recordings might provide a substi-
tute where the perceived ‘value’ of redevelopment was seen to exceed the ‘value’ of
preservation (Jowell 2005). Whilst at the time her suggestion was widely ridiculed
and effectively dismissed out of hand by the UK heritage community, it has long
been recognised that not everything can be preserved and digital methods of
recording heritage are already in use elsewhere. Barber et al. (2006) for example
discuss the use of digital recording in architectural conservation and Siu (2006)
describes its use in Hong Kong to record the historic Star Ferry Pier shortly before
its very controversial demolition to make way for redevelopment as part of a
harbour reclamation scheme.
The alternative dimensions of value also need to consider:
■	■ 	How heritage issues should impact upon land use and planning policy.
■	■ 	How large a proportion of society’s wealth and other resources should be set
aside and how these scarce resources should be allocated between competing
heritage demands.
■	■ 	How heritage resources should be controlled and managed in terms of use and
access.
■	■ 	How and to what extent society should impose controls upon the rights which
private property owners presently enjoy in order to ensure that ‘valuable’
heritage in private ownership is protected and preserved for public benefit.
It must be remembered that it is not simply the significance of individual buildings
which is important. Although many inner-city buildings have been preserved in

-- 303 of 346 --

286 	Chapter 15
isolation, modern thinking now recognises that in many cases the true value of an
asset, the context of a historic structure, can only be properly understood if it is
interpreted either as an integral part of a larger group or as an element in a wider
designed landscape.
The importance of cultural significance
The cultural significance of historic assets is a major constituent of value but is
extremely difficult to quantify. It is often related not so much to the financial value
of assets themselves or their embodied value in terms of their construction but is
more to do with their aesthetic value or their symbolic value as repositories of
history and memory, a concept neatly encapsulated in the phrase ‘buildings are
where we store our memories’ (MacIntyre 2010). Essentially, a historic asset’s
cultural significance is its ability to satisfy the needs of the human soul – what we
might term in modern language as the ‘feel-good factor’.
Clark (2001) picks up the notion of cultural significance in the context of the
development of historic assets when she writes that:
Significance lies at the heart of any conservation action – which for the
historic environment means the recognition of a public value in what may
well be private property. Historic buildings and their landscapes are signifi-
cant for many different cultural reasons: for their architecture, for their asso-
ciation with people and memories, beliefs and events or simply because they
are old. . . . That significance may be personal, local, regional, national or
international; it may be academic, economic or social. These values can be
roughly grouped into academic or scientific values and amenity values, such
as the way in which communities value places or the contribution a place
makes to the quality of life including economic value.
Cultural significance is not a simple idea. Rather it is a complex and multi-
faceted concept which may well mean different things to different cultural or socio-
economic groups. It has, in some cases, become almost synonymous with the
concept of ‘collective memory’ but it is important to differentiate between the two.
The ‘collective memory’ of a community may form part of the cultural significance
of a heritage asset, perhaps in some extreme cases a major element, but it will rarely
if ever be the only aspect of cultural significance to be worthy of consideration. The
multi-faceted nature of cultural significance has led some to develop formal typo-
logies which attempt to identify, and in some cases assign weightings to, the indi-
vidually identified components; these issues are addressed at length by Worthing
and Bond (2008).
In terms of the objective assessment of cultural significance, Clark (2001) points
out that in a national or international context recognition of the asset through listing
or scheduling provides some indication of the measure of importance (the ‘value’)
but she also points out that the significance may be geographically local, or may only
apply to some particular, perhaps minority, community group. Debates over the

-- 304 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	287
future of such assets are frequently extremely difficult to resolve satisfactorily,
particularly when different stakeholders hold different definitions of the cultural
significance associated with a particular asset, each of which is equally worthy in its
own way, which may be diametrically opposed. The issue of ‘cultural value’ is also
taken up, perhaps surprisingly, by economist David Throsby (2001, 2006), who
argues that cultural assets (including heritage assets) are special because they
embody cultural values which, in terms of their contribution to humanity, may well
exceed their economic or financial value. He characterises cultural value as compris-
ing the sum of aesthetic value, spiritual value, social value (a sense of identity and
place), historical value, symbolic value and authenticity. If this interpretation is
accepted, then we must also accept that a purely economic evaluation of a heritage
asset may not always be appropriate in representing its actual ‘worth’. This point of
view is supported by Professor Randall Mason (2005), who writes that:
The value of historic preservation need not be expressed and analysed only in
quantitative terms. Qualitative expressions of the value of preservation often
are dismissed by economists simply because they are not susceptible to stand-
ard economic (mathematically driven) methods of analysis. But these cultural
values – resisting easy quantification and mathematical treatment – are essen-
tial to the nature of historic preservation and they must somehow remain part
of the discourse on decision-making and other economic discourses on pres-
ervation. In other words, applying standard quantitative, market-derived
measures of historic preservation will not suffice – a priori – to express the
full value of preservation as cultural expression and public good.
The issue of how to define ‘the value of heritage’ is one which is now at the
forefront of the debate about how heritage should be managed and funded in the
future. The issue of cultural value, and how it should be assessed, is central to this
debate and experts appear to be unsure of how this should be done. Clark (2006)
and Smith (2010), amongst others, present a range of observations on different
aspects of the problem.
Statutory protection for heritage buildings
Many countries attempt to protect selected elements of their built environment
heritage through a variety of scheduling or listing regimes. In the UK, as in many
other countries, a distinction is made between listed buildings and scheduled mon-
uments. Any work carried out to a listed building, to a scheduled monument, or
within a conservation area will normally require that consent is obtained before
work begins.
In the UK, the process of obtaining consent normally requires application to the
local planning authority or to the relevant heritage authority (English Heritage,
Historic Scotland or Cadw in Wales) prior to the commencement of work. Failure
to obtain the necessary consents before beginning work is a criminal offence. There
are also a number of statutory amenity societies which must be consulted about

-- 305 of 346 --

288 	Chapter 15
planning applications that affect buildings or landscapes in which they have a
special interest. These include:
■	■ 	The Ancient Monuments Society, founded in 1924 for the study and conserva-
tion of ancient monuments, historic buildings and fine old craftsmanship.
■	■ 	The Georgian Group, dedicated to preserving Georgian buildings and gardens.
■	■ 	The Victorian Society, dedicated to the preservation of Victorian and Edwardian
architecture.
■	■ 	The Twentieth Century Society, dedicated to safeguarding architecture and
design in Britain from 1914 onwards.
■	■ 	The Garden History Society, dedicated to the conservation of historic designed
gardens and landscapes.
Listed buildings
Listed buildings are buildings which are considered to be of such significance that
they may be classified as being of national special interest. The major criteria for
listing in the UK are:
■	■ 	Age and rarity: Virtually all buildings dating from before 1700 which survive in
anything like their original condition are listed, as are most dating from between
1700 and 1840. A smaller percentage of late-nineteenth and twentieth century
buildings are also listed. Buildings must be more than ten years old to be eligi-
ble for listing and any building constructed within the previous 30 years would
have to be of exceptional importance.
■	■ 	Architectural interest: This category comprises buildings considered to be
exceptionally important because of their architecture, decoration or craftsman-
ship, or because they represent important advances in materials or techniques.
■	■ 	Historic interest: These are buildings closely associated with nationally impor-
tant people or events connected with the nation’s social, economic, cultural or
military history.
■	■ 	Group value: These buildings are important because they form part of a par-
ticularly important architectural or historic group or are excellent examples of
urban planning.
In England decisions as to which buildings should be listed are taken by the
Department of Culture Media and Sport with the advice of English Heritage.
Similar functions are devolved to the Scottish Parliament advised by Historic
Scotland and the Welsh Assembly advised by Cadw. Buildings on the list are graded
as follows:
■	■ 	Grade I buildings (about 2.5% of the total) are of exceptional interest, some-
times considered to be internationally important.
■	■ 	Grade II* buildings (approximately 5.5% of the total) are particularly impor-
tant buildings of more than special interest.
■	■ 	Grade II buildings (approximately 92%) are nationally important and of special
interest.

-- 306 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	289
Listed Building Consent must be obtained from the local planning authority
prior to the commencement of any work to a listed building. This may be a time-
consuming process and preliminary discussions at an early stage with the local
authority Conservation Officer are strongly recommended. There is a statutory
21-day period for consultation with amenity societies and other interested parties.
Local authorities generally aim to reach a decision on smaller schemes within
eight weeks but larger schemes are likely to take much longer. Where the project
involves demolition of all or part of a listed building or includes work to a Grade I
or II* building (also some classes of Grade II building in London) then the appli-
cation will be passed to English Heritage, Historic Scotland or Cadw for their
expert advice.
Conservation areas
Conservation areas in the UK are defined as areas of special architectural and
historic interest. They include:
■	■ 	Historic town and city centres.
■	■ 	Historic industrial or fishing communities.
■	■ 	Eighteenth and nineteenth century suburbs.
■	■ 	‘Model’ housing estates.
■	■ 	Country houses set in historic parks.
■	■ 	The route of historic infrastructure such as canals and railways.
In general, conservation areas are designated by local councils and their purpose is
to protect specific areas of historic or architectural interest from inappropriate
development or alteration. Any work within a conservation area, including altera-
tions or extensions to properties, whether or not they are individually listed, tree
cutting and the demolition of any structure will usually require the granting of
Conservation Area Consent prior to the commencement of work.
Scheduled monuments
Scheduled monuments are defined as sites or structures of national importance,
and are recorded on the Schedule of Monuments compiled and maintained by the
UK Government (in England), the Scottish Parliament (in Scotland) or the Welsh
Assembly (in Wales) under the provisions of the Ancient Monuments and
Archaeological Areas Act 1979. Anyone wishing to carry out any work to, or which
is likely to affect, a scheduled monument must first obtain Scheduled Monument
Consent from English Heritage, Historic Scotland or Cadw acting on behalf of the
Secretary of State for Culture, Media and Sport.
World Heritage Sites
Sites considered to be of worldwide importance are catalogued by the United
Nations Educational, Cultural and Scientific Organisation (UNESCO) as World

-- 307 of 346 --

290 	Chapter 15
Heritage Sites. At the time of writing there are 24 such sites in the UK, 20 of which
are historic buildings. UK World Heritage sites are administered by the Department
of Culture, Media and Sport and all of the buildings included are also either listed
or scheduled as historic monuments in their own right.
Managing change in the historic environment
It is clear that, in order to develop a workable procurement strategy for a scheme
which involves work to a historic building or a historic landscape, we must demon-
strate that an acceptable balance can be achieved between meeting the needs of the
client and protecting the interests of other stakeholders. The interests of the client
will usually be based upon a commercial decision and achieving the optimum
result from the client’s point of view will impose both time and cost constraints; in
the case of work to a historic building or a historic environment these factors will
themselves be constrained by the demands of the historic asset itself. These may
not be purely financial and need not necessarily be negative.
English Heritage guidance (Clark 2001) states that the value of a heritage asset
should be made explicit through an evidence-based conservation plan or conser-
vation statement and that this will normally be regarded as an essential support to
any competitive application for funding from organisations such as the Heritage
Lottery Fund.
Planning for conservation
It has already been established that a clear understanding of the history, value and
significance of a particular heritage building or site is an essential prerequisite to
the commissioning of any construction work which will affect it. Indeed Worthing
and Bond (2008) assert that it is only on the basis of this information that an accept-
able management strategy for the asset may be developed. Any subsequent work to
that asset, be it periodic repair and maintenance, restoration or reconstruction or
future change of use may then be procured in such a way as to ensure minimum
impact upon those key attributes of value and significance.
Prior to procuring any significant interventions to a heritage asset, a conserva-
tion plan or conservation statement will be required. A conservation plan is a
detailed document which may be very time consuming to prepare. It is essential for
some buildings but may not be appropriate for less important structures. An out-
line or ‘skeleton’ conservation plan, usually called a conservation statement, may be
sufficient for some buildings.
Conservation plans
A conservation plan is defined by Kerr (2004) as ‘a document which sets out what
is significant in the place and, consequently, what policies are appropriate to enable
that significance to be retained in its future use and development’. It is important to

-- 308 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	291
note that conservation plans should not be seen as essentially negative, placing
unnecessary constraints on the future use of a building or site. A conservation plan
is usually prepared in the knowledge that assets will evolve over time and, as
Worthing and Bond (2008) point out, ‘the challenge, which the conservation plan
must address, is how to manage change whilst protecting and enhancing that which
is embodied in and represented by the site – that is its cultural significance’. Clark
(2001) is even more explicit in stressing the positive aspects, when she writes that
‘understanding a building at risk can help identify constraints and opportunities
and thus contribute to bringing buildings back into use’ (emphasis added). A conser-
vation plan involves:
■	■ 	Research to identify the building’s value.
■	■ 	Assessment and articulation of the relative significance of its value from the
point of view of all the stakeholders involved.
■	■ 	Assessment of the extent to which its value may be vulnerable.
■	■ 	The formulation of potential strategies, policies and practices which will miti-
gate those vulnerabilities, whilst safeguarding the future use of the asset.
Conservation statements
A full conservation plan is essential for exceptionally significant buildings (for
example all buildings listed as Grade I, all Scheduled Monuments and most build-
ings listed as Grade II*). In contrast, a conservation statement may be sufficient for
buildings of lesser importance. A conservation statement uses readily available
information sources to formulate a statement of the site’s cultural significance,
identify major conservation issues and formulate an outline policy for the develop-
ment of the site.
Asset management plans
A conservation plan or conservation statement forms the foundation for the
development of an asset management plan. A management plan develops the
policies and processes outlined in the conservation plan or statement and uses
them to inform and develop further processes designed to manage the asset in
the future, usually through an on-going programme of preventative maintenance,
repair, conservation, reconstruction or adaptive reuse. It is a fact of life that, in
the modern world, the majority of heritage buildings must ‘pay their way’ and
that finding new uses for old structures, perhaps even, in extremis, partial or
complete demolition of some elements, may be necessary in order to secure the
survival of more important elements of the structure. It should therefore also be
noted that it is an important function of the management plan to address and
resolve any difficulties and conflicts which may arise between the often rather
altruistic conservation plan considerations and the harder-edged and more con-
troversial financial and commercial considerations implicit in the management
plan objectives.

-- 309 of 346 --

292 	Chapter 15
Heritage Impact Assessments
A further important tool in the process of assessing the impact of change on the
historic environment is the Heritage Impact Assessment (HIA). An HIA, which
may be seen as analogous to an Environmental Impact Assessment, is usually pre-
pared in parallel with the initial conservation plan, and developed and extended in
parallel with the asset management plan in the event of specific proposals for major
interventions to, or adaptive reuse of, the site. It formally assesses the impact (pos-
itive as well as negative) which proposed changes may have upon the significant
historical and cultural values associated with the building or its environment.
Comparative HIAs prepared to assess the relative impacts of a number of alterna-
tive schemes may form a useful component of project option appraisal and risk
management strategies.
The planning process
Clark (2001) sets out a detailed formal methodology for preparing conservation
plans and conservation statements and for carrying out Heritage Impact
Assessments within the broader Conservation Based Research and Assessment
(CoBRA) framework based upon the following key stages:
(1) 	Establish the need and justification.
(2) 	Specify the work.
(3) 	Define the significance of the building or site.
(4) 	Establish the impact of the proposals upon the significance.
(5) 	Mitigate or reduce the impact to acceptable levels.
A conservation statement or full conservation plan, together with an asset
management plan and an HIA, may be required as part of an overall Environmental
Impact Assessment, as part of an application for funding by national bodies (such
as the Heritage Lottery Fund) or international funding organisations (such as the
World Bank), as part of a consultation process or as part of an application for the
granting of consent (whether planning, scheduled monument, listed building or
conservation area consent) by a local authority or national body. The decision as to
whether a conservation statement will suffice or whether a full conservation plan is
required would need to be agreed at an early stage between the project conserva-
tion advisor, where one is appointed, and the appropriate statutory bodies.
Processes such as those outlined above are potentially both time consuming and
expensive. Due allowance will therefore need to be made by the procurement advi-
sor, both in decisions regarding appropriate procurement methodologies and in
any pre-contract project budgets and programmes.
The role of the conservation advisor
Conservation advisors are typically employed either by a body responsible for
granting permission for planned work to take place (for example a local authority)

-- 310 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	293
or as a member of the project team by those seeking to obtain such permission. In
either case the work which they carry out will be similar.
It has already been established that specialist skills and advice are required
whenever any work is to be carried out to a historic structure; the procurement
team should therefore include relevant specialist conservation expertise. Where a
traditional design-led process is envisaged, it would be common to select an archi-
tect specialising in this type of work, but if this is not the case then a consultant
conservation advisor, typically a chartered surveyor with conservation experience
or a member of the Institute of Historic Building Conservation, should be consid-
ered. In the case of larger schemes or particularly significant buildings, it is also
desirable that other members of the consultant team, for example the quantity sur-
veyor and the structural and mechanical services engineers, also have some back-
ground in working with heritage buildings.
In the early stages of a project, the central role of the conservation advisor will
be to provide advice and guidance to the client and the project team in respect of
the important historical and cultural aspects of the structure and its environment.
It is therefore likely that the conservation advisor may lead the team preparing
the conservation plan or conservation statement, will subsequently advise the cli-
ent and his team in the preparation and consideration of commercial option
appraisals relating to possible future projects, and will be a member of the team
which prepares any subsequent asset management plan or Heritage Impact
Assessment.
As the project develops, it is likely that the conservation advisor will be retained:
■	■ 	To advise on any negotiations with interested parties such as the local authority
conservation officer, English Heritage, statutory consultees and other stake-
holders.
■	■ 	To provide specialist input to design and specification issues.
■	■ 	To oversee any pre-contract building or site investigations.
■	■ 	To advise on risk or contingency allowances to be included in any project
documentation.
■	■ 	To advise upon the selection and appointment of consultants and contractors
to undertake the work.
Post contract, the conservation advisor may be retained to provide advice as
required on any issues of a historical or archaeological nature which may arise dur-
ing the construction process. The role of the conservation advisor is discussed in
detail by Clark (2001).
Procurement processes and procedures
It has already been established that work to heritage buildings and sites is generally
subject to much greater risk and uncertainty than more conventional projects and
there is a danger that this may lead to significant time and cost risks both at tender
stage and during the construction period. It is therefore much more difficult to
manage these projects such that business plan objectives, in particular value for

-- 311 of 346 --

294 	Chapter 15
money (however that may be defined), are achieved. In the past this problem has
frequently been addressed simply by including higher than normal contingency
sums or by including provisional sum allowances for unforeseen work. Neither of
these approaches is particularly effective.
In an attempt to limit risk exposure on heritage projects, some organisations
who regularly work in this area have developed standardised procedures for the
procurement of projects of this type. One good example is the procedure developed
by the government of the Republic of Ireland (Irish Government 2011). Many of
the risks may be minimised by adopting a strategy in which the project is divided
into two separate contractual packages:
■	■ 	An investigation contract designed to obtain sufficient information to allow
comprehensive designs and specifications for the work to be prepared.
■	■ 	A works contract under which the construction work may be carried out.
Procurement of the works contract is likely to be straightforward, but particular
care may need to be taken with the investigation contract. It is likely that common
sense will dictate that some minor elements of the permanent construction work
may need to be included within the investigation contract, particularly when the
investigation contract includes items for opening up and exposing elements of the
structure. It is however important to ensure that there is a clear division between
the two contracts, with the majority of the construction work being carried out
under in the main contract.
The investigation contract
The purpose of the investigation contract is to allow comprehensive designs and
specifications for the main construction work to be prepared. Thus, it should limit
the risk of time and cost over-runs on the main contract. Depending upon the type
of project, the scale and scope of the work required may be very varied, ranging
from desk-based evaluations to complex underground archaeology or a complete
and detailed archaeological survey of an existing structure. English Heritage
(1994a) provides a useful guide to the building investigation process and Barber
et al. (2008) present a comprehensive good practice guide to managing archaeology
within the development process.
The type of field work involved may range from the excavation of trial pits
through the opening up of inaccessible areas of a structure for examination and
permanently or temporarily making good elements which have been disturbed to
a full scale archaeological survey of a site or structure. The costs involved may be
considerable; Heaton (2007), for example, writes that archaeological excavations at
the Bath Spa site cost some £500 000 at 2007 price levels.
The investigation contract is therefore a stand-alone contract in its own right
and may need to be the subject of a stand-alone procurement exercise, the scope
and scale of which will be largely dependent upon the type of work likely to be
required. Heaton (2007) states that, historically, even the largest of archaeological
contracts have been let using rudimentary documentation often using a simple

-- 312 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	295
lump sum contract. He makes a powerful case for such projects to be let using
remeasurement contracts based upon a schedule of rates to provide improved cost
transparency.
Plainly for small contracts, a simple contract such as the JCT minor works form,
suitably amended, might be sufficient, but for larger projects a more complex form
such as the JCT Intermediate Contract or the NEC3 Short Form, again suitably
amended and accompanied by a Schedule of Rates listing the range of work items
expected to arise might be a better choice.
In 2004, the Institution of Civil Engineers published the ICE Conditions of
Contract for Archaeological Investigation to provide for archaeological excavations
in connection with civil engineering works. The form has now been rebadged as
the Infrastructure Conditions of Contract Archaeological Investigation Version 2011
and is currently published jointly by the ACE and the CECA.
A case study in the procurement of work to historic
buildings: The Derby roundhouse
The purpose of this case study is to illustrate how some of the issues and difficulties
associated with the procurement of construction works to historic structures may
be addressed. With the exception of the historical background, the case study has
been compiled largely from information provided by the project architect, Ian
Harris of Maber Architects; the project quantity surveyor, Steve Fernie of Armsons;
and Mike Atkinson of the contractor, Bowmer and Kirkland.
The case study concerns the rehabilitation and adaptive reuse of a group of
iconic buildings, remnants of the former Midland Railway’s Derby Locomotive
Works, later the British Rail Engineering Ltd (BREL) Derby Locomotive Works.
The buildings became redundant following the closure and demolition of the
majority of the site in the early 1990s. The remaining buildings comprise the
roundhouse (Figure 15.1) and part of the associated workshops, originally built for
the North Midland Railway, together with a detached engine house and workshop
building, originally built for the Midland Counties Railway.
A number of attempts were made over a period of some 20 years to find alterna-
tive uses for the buildings, none of which were successful, and the buildings conse-
quently languished on the English Heritage Buildings at Risk register from the date
of its first publication. By the early 2000s, when the present scheme was first initi-
ated, the buildings had suffered fire damage and were in a serious state of decay
(Figure 15.2).
Finally, in late 2007, construction work began to rescue the buildings and they
now form the core of the Derby College city campus. The completed project was
chosen by English Heritage as one of 20 exemplary projects (English Heritage
2008), was awarded a Heritage Award by the East Midlands Centre for Constructing
the Built Environment in 2009, and was chosen by the Royal Institution of
Chartered Surveyors as Project of the Year 2010.

-- 313 of 346 --

296 	Chapter 15
Figure 15.1 	The Derby roundhouse.
Figure 15.2 	The interior of the roundhouse prior to commencement of the contract.
(Source: Maber Architects.)

-- 314 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	297
The existing buildings: historical background
The Derby roundhouse together with its associated engine and carriage workshops,
was built for the North Midland Railway as a part of the ‘Tri-Junct’ station scheme
(Higginson 1989) to provide joint railway facilities at Derby for the North Midland,
Midland Counties and Birmingham and Derby Junction railway companies. The
station complex – comprising the station building, the adjoining Midland Hotel
(for first-class passengers), the Brunswick Inn (for second-class passengers), the
roundhouse and attached workshops, warehousing and workers housing – was
designed for the North Midland Railway by Robert Stephenson, joint engineer to
the railway with his father George, and the very talented railway architect Francis
Thompson (Biddle 1986; Simmons 1991). The complex was built by Thomas
Jackson of Pimlico, London (Billson 1996) and opened in 1840 to considerable
critical acclaim: the contemporary critic Francis Whishaw (1842) wrote of:
the admirably contrived and elegant roofs, the spacious platforms, the great
length of the whole erection extending to upwards of a thousand feet, – all
unite in rendering it the most complete structure of the kind in the United
Kingdom, or, perhaps, in the world.
The Midland Counties and Birmingham and Derby Junction railway companies
each built their own maintenance facilities on the site and paid a fee to the North
Midland for the use of the station until all three companies were merged to form
the Midland Railway in 1844. Sadly, the main station building was, somewhat con-
troversially, redeveloped between 1952 and 1985 and the facilities built by the
Birmingham and Derby Junction Railway have also been demolished.
The North Midland roundhouse and workshops were designed in 1839 and
constructed in 1839–40 at a cost of £62 000 (Glover 1843). Trains ran from a
temporary platform until the main station was completed. Robert Stephenson
was responsible for the basic engineering layout of the station complex but he
appointed Francis Thompson to carry out the architectural work on the line, a
collaboration which continued for many years on subsequent schemes. The
design of the station complex was therefore the result of collaboration between
Thompson as architect and Stephenson as engineer. Billson (1996) attributes the
design of all of the North Midland buildings on the site to Francis Thompson.
The station and the Midland Hotel lie on one side of the main running lines and
the roundhouse complex on the other. The three were linked by an underground
tunnel several hundred yards long in order to provide safe passage without cross-
ing the running lines. At some time prior to 1880, the tunnel was supplanted by
an overhead footbridge from the station which ran into the roundhouse at first
floor level above the main entrance door. The opening cut into the existing arch
and the cast iron beam inserted to carry the end of the bridge are both clearly
visible in Figure 15.3. The bridge was later removed and was replaced with a
more modern structure which came to ground level outside the roundhouse
complex. The tunnel was blocked some years ago, but the entrances to the tunnel
still existed at the commencement of the present scheme. The entrance to the

-- 315 of 346 --

298 	Chapter 15
roundhouse complex, which was located in the basement of the old engine shed
wing, was blocked off in the course of this contract.
The polygonal roundhouse was one of three built between 1839 and 1840, all on
lines engineered by the Stephensons, the other two being at Miles Platting on the
Leeds and Manchester Railway and at Curzon Street station in Birmingham on the
London and Birmingham Railway. All are described in varying degrees of detail by
Whishaw (1842), himself a professional civil engineer, and all appear to have been
of a similar form. Like Derby, Curzon Street is said to have 16 lines of rail with
space for 30 engines and they are of a similar size (Curzon Street being 124 feet in
diameter as against Derby’s 130 feet). The major difference between the two seems
to be that on the Curzon Street building the central portion was left unroofed, a
situation which Wishaw considered very inconvenient and which prompted him to
comment that ‘a sky-light with proper means of ventilation would . . . answer the
purpose much better’. The fact that the Derby building had both skylights and a
ventilated lantern would appear to point to this being a development from the ear-
lier Curzon Street design. Whishaw plainly considers the roundhouse design to be
an evolving standard, simply describing the Miles Platting building as ‘of polygonal
form, according to Mr. Stephenson’s usual plan’. The Curzon Street and Miles
Platting buildings have both been demolished, leaving Derby as the oldest surviv-
ing roundhouse in the world.
In fact the construction of circular workshops laid out in a radial fashion was
well known at the time the early roundhouses were designed. One of the best
Figure 15.3 	Main entrance to the roundhouse offices.

-- 316 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	299
documented is the Round Foundry constructed in Leeds about 1802–03 by Fenton,
Murray and Wood. This building is described in a letter accompanied by a sketch
written by James Watt Jr in the course of an industrial espionage expedition to
Leeds in 1803 (Leeds Engine Builders 2012):
Fame has not outdone the magnitude of Murray’s new Edifice, it is a
rotundo of about 100 feet in diameter with a magnificent Entrance. The
Engine is to stand in the middle and the lower rooms to serve as deposits
for Engines & other finished goods. The Upper rooms to be for fitting. It
is an excellent building & will not look amiss. It is up to the top of the 2nd
storey.
Fenton, Murray and Wood built railway locomotives at the Round Foundry
from the very earliest days of railway development, supplying the first two locomo-
tives for Blenkinsop’s Middleton rack-railway in 1811–12 and building engines to
George and Robert Stephenson’s designs from 1831 (Grace’s Guide 2012). Both
George and Robert Stephenson would certainly have known the Round Foundry
well and one might reasonably hypothesise that it, or something like it, acted as the
inspiration for their later buildings.
The Derby roundhouse was plainly seen as something of a local wonder and
excited detailed comment in tourist guide books of the time (see for example the
work of Allen and Allen 1840). Glover (1843) described it in the following terms:
The engine-houses and workshops . . . of the North Midland are very taste-
fully and admirably constructed. The engine-house is a polygon of sixteen
sides, and 130 feet in diameter, and from the floor to the top of the lantern,
48 feet 10 inches, viz. the height of columns 18 feet, roof 23 feet, and lantern,
7 feet 10 inches. The dome or conical roof is lighted by 48 windows. The
exterior covering is of slate laid upon board, firmly screwed to the roof
beams. Its spacious area will contain more than thirty engines, which may
be moved with the greatest facility to any part of the building, by means of
16 lines of rails radiating from a central turntable. To the right are the engine
workshops, 184 feet 6 inches long, 70 feet 6 inches wide, the truss beams of
the floor supported by 20 massive cast iron columns; the smithy attached
is 42 feet long, of the same width. To the left are the carriage workshops,
191 feet 6 inches, by 70 feet 6 inches, the truss beams of the floor of the
largest are supported by 16 cast iron columns. These buildings form oblique
wings to the polygon.
Whishaw (1842) adds that ‘there are two entry-buildings or lobbies, over which are
tanks of water for the supply of the locomotives. . . . Abutting on each of the
lobbies . . . are the turneries, smithy, and other workshops’. The lobbies are plainly
apparent in later plans of the Derby Works. What is claimed to be a contemporary
drawing of the Curzon Street building (Warwickshire Railways 2012) appears to
show a similar single lobby and water tank arrangement. The central turntable,
originally hand-cranked but converted to electrical operation many years ago, is
still in situ and is preserved in the completed scheme.

-- 317 of 346 --

300 	Chapter 15
Billson (1996) writes that the original Thompson scheme included single-storey
works offices linking the two workshop wings, surmounted by a stone campanile.
It is known (Smith and Groom 2010) that the John Whitehurst clock was built in
1840 and that the clock face was originally located in the circular opening at the top
of the campanile. Billson records that, in 1859–60, the office buildings were raised
by an additional storey and a timber cap surmounted by a weather-vane was placed
on the top of the clock tower. The office block was raised by a further storey in 1883
and the timber cap to the clock tower was removed and the clock and the original
weather-vane were re-sited in the present timber housing. All of these alterations
are clearly evident in Figure 15.3. The somewhat incongruous change in roofline
across the top of the campanile is believed to be due to replacement of the roof to
what remained of the right wing following a fire in the 1950s.
The roundhouse continued to be used as a repair facility until the mid-1980s, at
which time it was being used by BREL for the refurbishment of large rail-mounted
cranes. After that it seems to have been used as a store.
The roundhouse, the office building and what remains of the ‘oblique wings’
described by Glover are clearly seen in Figure 15.4. The carriage shop to the left
of the clock tower is clearly visible but the engine shop to the right was demol-
ished after being destroyed by fire in 1950. A plan of the Derby Locomotive Works
dated 1950 clearly shows that the engine shop was, by then, being used for ‘Loco
Stores’ and the carriage shop was being used as the millwright’s shop (loose_
grip_99 2012). The roundhouse and carriage workshop are listed Grade II*
(English Heritage 1977a, 1994b) and the clock tower is listed Grade II (English
Heritage 1977b).
Figure 15.4 	Aerial view of the completed scheme. (Source: Bowmer and Kirkland Ltd.)

-- 318 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	301
The Midland Counties engine shed and workshop building is the one- and two-
storey red brick structure shown in Figure 15.5. It is now linked to the roundhouse
complex by the new Kirtley building. The engineer for the Midland Counties line
was Charles Blacker Vignoles; Billson (1996) records that the tender specifications
for this building were issued from the engineer’s office in Leicester in April 1839.
He provisionally attributes the design of the building to a Leicester architect,
William Parsons, who was responsible for the design of the company’s Leicester
station.
Glover (1843) was somewhat less impressed by this building than by the round-
house, describing it merely as ‘a spacious engine shed 134 feet by 52 feet, and work-
shops 200 feet by 93 feet, the repairing shop is 93 feet by 88 feet; these are
substantial brick buildings’. A comparison of Glover’s description with Figure 15.4
will show that this building has also been substantially altered since it was first
constructed and this is confirmed by the English Heritage list description (English
Heritage 1994c) which grades the building as II* and describes it as a ‘railway
workshop incorporating former beam engine house, fitting and turning shops
and smithies, latterly pattern shop and store . . . with C19 and C20 alterations and
additions’.
The North Midland, Midland Counties and Birmingham and Derby Junction
Railways merged in 1844 to form the Midland Railway. It made Derby its
headquarters and the Derby complex became the Midland Railway Locomotive
Figure 15.5 	The Midland Counties Railway Engine Shed and Workshop with the new
Stephenson building in the background. (Source: Maber Architects.)

-- 319 of 346 --

302 	Chapter 15
Works. Expansion of the works meant that by 1876 production of carriages and
rolling stock was transferred to the new Derby Litchurch Lane works, leaving the
original works to become a major centre of locomotive building and maintenance
for the next 100 years.
The genesis of the project
By mid-2002 the majority of the old locomotive works site was already in the early
stages of redevelopment as Pride Park, a major new business and leisure hub cen-
tred around the new Derby County Pride Park football stadium. The roundhouse
site, seen as a ‘flagship’ site in Derby Cityscape’s master plan to bring £2 billion
pounds worth of investment to Derby city centre, was proving very problematic
because of the need to find a new use which would both protect and preserve the
important listed buildings and would also permit some degree of public access.
The opportunity to use the site for educational purposes arose out of the creation
in 2002 of Derby College from the merger of three existing colleges, Wilmorton
College, Mackworth College and the Broomfield Hall Agricultural College. The
newly created vocational college needed a city centre location close to existing
transport links and the college principal, David Croll, took the very brave decision
to redevelop the roundhouse site; he became the project’s ‘prime mover’. The site
was seen as a particularly appropriate choice firstly because the vocational nature
of courses to be delivered on the site (including engineering, construction, com-
puting and art and design) echoed the site’s industrial history and secondly because
of the site’s strong community links embodied in the memories of the many former
workers still living in the city.
Maber Architects, who already had an established reputation as conservation
architects, were appointed in 2003, having completed a previous scheme for the
college. English Heritage were involved from the beginning of the scheme and
report (English Heritage 2008) that they were:
confident about the ability of the architects and . . . [were] . . . from the start,
supportive of the overarching vision for the site . . . [helping] . . . the local
authority to feel similarly confident that the scheme was going to succeed.
A conservation plan and building surveys had been prepared for the site several
years previously and English Heritage (2008) report that the project passed
smoothly through the planning process. The ground surrounding the buildings
had already been excavated to a depth of some 500 mm as part of the wider demoli-
tion and remediation contract for the site as a whole and there was, therefore, no
need for a further archaeological survey.
The final scheme
The brief for the new development was, in essence, deceptively simple – to provide
approximately 18 500 m 2 of twenty-first century vocational teaching and learning
space for approximately 2500 students, whilst protecting and preserving as much as

-- 320 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	303
possible of the existing listed buildings. In reality, pre- tender processes including
development of the brief, project design and securing the necessary approvals and
funding for the project took almost four years.
Modern conservation practice holds that interventions to historic fabric should
be kept to a minimum and that modern interventions and repairs to existing build-
ings should, in general be clearly distinguishable from the original work. The archi-
tects for this scheme have attempted wherever possible to do this by following the
William Morris principle of ‘honest repair’, with no attempt being made either to
disguise the repairs or to make them look like anything other than a modern
intervention (see Figure 15.6).
Wherever possible, for example in the case of all brickwork repairs, the work was
carried out using reclaimed bricks from elsewhere on the site; where it was not pos-
sible, for example in the case of the re-slated roof to the roundhouse, considerable
efforts have been made to source reclaimed replacements which match as closely as
possible those used in the original building. In only a few instances has it been
necessary to intervene to improve, rather than repair, the historic structure. The
issue of restoration of missing elements is also a vexed question. Contemporary
thought is that, particularly with important listed structures, the restoration of
missing features should be approached with great care and, in general terms, imita-
tion of historic features should not be attempted unless there is clear evidence of
the form of the original.
Both of these issues were relevant in the case of the roundhouse roof, where
the architect proposed to reinstate the missing lantern to the apex of the roof,
which had been removed some years earlier. Historic photographs provided suf-
ficient detail for English Heritage and the conservation officer to agree to the
restoration but computer modelling of the existing rather unconventional
Figure 15.6 	‘Honest repair’ – no attempt is made to make the repair look like anything
other than a modern intervention.

-- 321 of 346 --

304 	Chapter 15
inverted timber truss structure, allegedly designed by Robert Stephenson himself
and therefore of exceptional historic significance, revealed that the design was
inadequate to carry the additional load. Additional timbers had therefore to be
introduced in positions approved by the conservation officer to make the roof
structurally sound.
A further issue concerns the reversal of earlier alterations. There is a strongly
held view by some conservation officers that alterations become part of the build-
ing’s life and history and should therefore be preserved. In the case of the round-
house office block, the timber windows to the second floor to the right of the clock
tower were replaced, probably after the 1950 fire, with much larger steel windows.
The architect considered it desirable, on aesthetic grounds, to remove the later win-
dows and reinstate the openings to their original size with new timber windows to
match those on the opposite side of the tower.
The final scheme uses the existing roundhouse to provide a ‘social hub’ and visi-
tor centre for the college. It also provides a space which can be used for external
functions (see Figure 15.7). The adjoining former carriage shop is now used as a
library and resource space and a multi-purpose theatre (Figure 15.8) and the origi-
nal Midland Railway building provides teaching space for catering and hospitality
courses (Figure 15.9) together with general office space. The original office build-
ing and clock tower, now named the Thompson building, contains office accom-
modation for administration staff.
Figure 15.7 	The interior of the refurbished roundhouse.

-- 322 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	305
Figure 15.8 	The interior of the old carriage shop. Note the prefabricated ‘learning
pods’ on the upper floor. (Source: Maber Architects.)
Figure 15.9 	The new training restaurant, with the training kitchen in the background,
in the old Midland Counties workshop building.

-- 323 of 346 --

306 	Chapter 15
It is a further fundamental principle of adaptive reuse that modern interventions
should be, as far as possible, reversible so that the building could be returned to its
original state if required in the future. The requirement for small differentiated
spaces (for example offices for teaching staff or seminar rooms) has therefore been
met through the provision of self-contained prefabricated ‘pods’, delivered to the
site complete with all services and finishings pre-installed and located within the
existing spaces. The pods are designed to echo railway carriages (see Figure 15.8),
thus further reinforcing the character of the existing spaces.
The existing buildings are complemented and supplemented by two major new
structures, which aim to capture in a modern way, in terms of their form and struc-
ture, the spirit of the early railway pioneers. The Stephenson building (Figure 15.10)
provides 24 classrooms and 12 workshops dedicated to construction, hair and
beauty, and art courses. It was very carefully designed to provide maximum value
for money.
The Kirtley building, which links the old carriage shop and the Midland Railway
buildings, houses the college’s engineering department and is noteworthy for its
innovative use of ‘chameleon glass’ technology. The glazed curtain walling to the
front of the building is coated with a dichroic film, which enables the glazing to
change colour depending upon the angle of view (Figure 15.11).
The architects, supported by the consultant quantity surveyor and a number of
key specialist companies, conducted a rigorous value management exercise as a
part of the design process for the Stephenson and Kirtley buildings, which deliv-
ered substantial benefits. The Stephenson building achieved a BREAM Very Good
rating for an all-in cost of £1035 per square metre, compared to the Learning and
Skills Council norm at the time of £1235 per square metre.
Figure 15.10 	The new Stephenson building. (Source: Maber Architects.)
