Figure 15.11 	The new Kirtley building.
Figure 15.12 	Echoes of the building’s history captured in the decoration of the
training restaurant.

-- 325 of 346 --

308 	Chapter 15
The spirit of the original buildings is also perpetuated in the interior design,
which features wall-sized images of notable figures associated with the site and
representations of various aspects of steam railway technology (see Figure 15.12).
Site surveys and pre-tender investigations
The extended pre-contract period for this project meant that the architects had
time to complete detailed surveys of the existing structures before tenders were
invited. In addition to normal building surveys, a number of specialist surveys,
including a full asbestos survey, were also carried out.
As an example, detailed brickwork investigations were carried out in conjunc-
tion with the local conservation officer and a specialist masonry conservation con-
tractor. Detailed plans were formulated as to the methodologies to be used for
brickwork replacement and repair, including specially designed mortar mixes. All
of the existing brickwork was heavily stained with soot and defaced with modern
graffiti; careful trials were made, also in conjunction with the masonry contractor,
to determine the most appropriate methodology and specification for cleaning.
The architect reports that there was considerable discussion between himself and
the local conservation officer as to whether cleaning was to be permitted at all or
whether ‘the soot was listed’. The final decision, supported by English Heritage, was
that cleaning should be permitted, perhaps on the grounds that if it was not then it
was unlikely the adaptive reuse would go ahead at all. A similar approach was taken
in respect of the metal and timber windows. Specialist engineers Hutton and
Rostron carried out a detailed micro-core survey of every piece of remaining struc-
tural timber and the project consultant structural engineers prepared a detailed
schedule of timber repairs which was included in the tender documents.
Specialist contractors employed during this stage were paid a fee and no guaran-
tees were given as to whether or not they would be invited to tender for the actual
work.
The tender process
The tender process was conducted under OJEU rules, with a prequalification exer-
cise followed by a formal invitation to tender. Bids were invited from five contrac-
tors using a two-stage process based upon the JCT 98 Standard Building Contract
Without Quantities incorporating a Contractor’s Design Supplement for the
mechanical and electrical services. The unconventional approach adopted towards
the building services installations was brought forward with the intention of access-
ing value for money and market testing from design to installation, which was per-
ceived to be frequently lacking in a more traditional consultant-led design process.
The approach was not unanimously embraced by the project team; in post-
completion review it was agreed that it delivered fewer benefits than hoped for and
did not promote holistic solutions.
Tender documents comprised detailed drawings supported by full NBS specifi-
cations, specialist schedules and a detailed cost plan. Bids were submitted on a

-- 326 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	309
lump sum basis with a Tender Price Analysis based upon an annotated version of
the tender cost plan, with the contractor’s profit and overhead percentages declared,
supported by a detailed written submission.
The extended pre-contract period meant that by the time tenders were invited
the architects and quantity surveyor had a detailed knowledge of the existing build-
ings. Risk was managed at this stage through the use of defined Provisional Sums
for specific known risk items, supplemented by the inclusion of a contingency sum
worth approximately 8% of the predicted contract value. The contract documenta-
tion included no Prime Cost sums or defined subcontractors.
Assessment of stage one was carried out on a price–quality basis (40% price, 60%
quality). Following the assessment, which included an interview, the contract was
awarded to locally-based contractor with National focus, Bowmer and Kirkland.
Stage two of the pre-contract process involved a detailed negotiation with the cho-
sen contractor on the basis of a number of individual specialist work packages and
subcontractor quotations to fix the final contract sum.
The project team strongly believe that the traditional procurement approach was
the only way in which this very complex project could have been successfully com-
pleted. This view was supported by the Learning and Skills Council, who had
become very wary of the inflated and unnecessary pricing of risk in many well-
detailed higher education projects let using a pure design and build approach.
The partnering process
The project team, strongly led by the project architect, believed from the very
beginning of this project that it would only be successful if everyone working on
the project shared their own passion and commitment. They took the conscious
decision that rather than follow the conventional route of employing a partnering
facilitator, they would use the unique nature of the buildings, their own passion for
rehabilitating them, and the site’s strong resonance in the local community (a large
proportion of the local workforce had worked ‘at the Loco’ and still remembered it
with both pride and affection), as the ‘glue’ with which to bind the project delivery
team together.
Accordingly, before the contractor was appointed, the project architect devel-
oped a ‘toolbox talk’ based around a PowerPoint presentation which provided an
illustrated history of the site and the design journey. The presentation did not only
concentrate on the structures themselves but also provided an appreciation of the
wider contribution the original works had made to the development of transport
and the economic and social impact, both nationally and locally. This led into an
explanation of the resulting design generators, conservation approach, sensitivity
and opportunities that the project presented. The project architect believes very
strongly that the toolbox talks were a major element in getting all concerned with
project delivery to ‘buy in’, both to the architect’s design vision and also to idea of
rescuing the buildings themselves. The talks also gave a platform for subcontrac-
tors to pick up, suggest ideas and make contributions of their own which
undoubtedly added further strength and depth. The architect strongly believes that

-- 327 of 346 --

310 	Chapter 15
the shared enthusiasm and vision was very successful and that all concerned, on
and off the site, took a genuine pride in their work. As a result of this, the main
contractor’s contract manager confirms that the delivery team quickly developed a
true partnering spirit of trust and mutual co-operation, resulting in an atmosphere
where any disagreements which did arise were resolved quickly and amicably.
A formal partnering charter was considered unnecessary.
It is perhaps an indication of the trust which existed between the client, the con-
sultant team and the main contractor that, midway through the original 22-month
contract period, a deal was done under which the contractor was given the remain-
ing contingency pot in exchange for absorbing all remaining project risks with the
exception of client changes.
Post-contract processes
Post-contract processes generally followed those included in the Standard
Building Contract with the architect as contract administrator. Although the cli-
ent insisted that a project manager be appointed, there is of course no such formal
role in the JCT contract structure and the project manager’s role tended therefore
to be more of a project co-ordinator. Almost inevitably given the nature of the
work, Bowmer and Kirkland chose to subcontract most of the construction. Prices
for variations were submitted by the contractor based very largely upon subcon-
tractor quotations and were inclusive of all programme implications and the main
contractor’s profit and overheads at the rates declared in the tender. The prices
were then reviewed by the consultant team followed, if necessary, by negotiation
before final agreement.
One very significant variation occurred shortly before practical completion,
when the Learning and Skills Council asked the college to include a training kitchen
and restaurant within the scheme. Additional money was provided and it was
understood that an extension to the contract period would be required. The altera-
tion required substantial redesign of the interior of part of the old Midland Counties
Building, which had initially been designed to provide teaching space for brick-
work and plastering courses. It is perhaps a measure of the strength of the bond
between the members of the project team that the variation was incorporated and
the additional costs and the necessary extension to the contract period were agreed
very quickly and with no adverse contractual consequences.
Practical issues on site
Repairs to historic structures
Despite pre-contract surveys, it is virtually impossible in a project like this to accu-
rately assess the extent of the work required to the existing buildings until initial
opening-up works have been carried out. In the words of Mike Atkinson (Bowmer
and Kirkland’s contract manager), ‘it is very difficult if not impossible on a Project
such as The Roundhouse to “Know what you don’t know” ’ (Atkinson 2009).

-- 328 of 346 --

Procurement of work to heritage buildings 	311
This sentiment is no more true than in the case of the brickwork repairs.
Although the architect had been able to determine methodologies and specifica-
tions before the contract, the actual work to be done couldn’t be determined until
scaffolding was erected and the architects were able to inspect the walls at close
quarters. Work on site consisted of an architect spending the best part of a year
preparing detailed sketches of every elevation to show precisely the work required
in terms of the areas to be rebuilt, cut out and replaced or repointed.
Wherever possible, all materials for repairs were sourced from buildings demol-
ished on the site and the contractor established a site-based recycling yard. Where
it was not possible to reclaim materials, materials equal in specification to those
used in the original buildings were sourced from other demolition sites. Of the
waste generated by the project, only 17% went to landfill.
Integration of building services
The integration of the mechanical and electrical services necessary to provide
modern levels of environmental performance within listed buildings is always a
technical challenge; buildings as important as the roundhouse pose a particular
challenge. In this case, engineers made use of the existing ash pits beneath the
radial tracks in the floor to form air supply plenums. The pits were sealed at floor
level with timber panels incorporating diffuser grilles and ductwork was intro-
duced into the roundhouse basement to provide an air supply to the plenum cham-
bers. Extract ventilation was provided through opening rooflights linked to the
building management system. Electrical services are fed by surface-mounted
conduits routed along the top of the roof members.
Project timeframe, costs and team
The work on site started on 5th October 2007. The first phase handover (of the
roundhouse and the first-phase classrooms) took place in June 2009 and the con-
tract was completed on 13th July 2009.
The total value of the project was £48 million. Table 15.1 shows the cost break-
down of the project (this breakdown excludes furniture, fit-out and VAT). Included
in these costs are repairs to the brickwork of around £1 million; repairs to the wood-
work of around £2.5 million; and repairs to the roof slating of around £2 million.
Taking into account the additional funding provided for the incorporation of the
training kitchen and restaurant, the project finished on time and on budget.

16 Summary
This book has highlighted the importance of construction to quality of life and to
the economy of a country from the procurement of large-scale public and
commercial works through to the heritage sector. The sheer breadth and scale of
the construction industry gives rise to a diverse array of procurement approaches
in order to cope with the diversity of demand and the complexities of supply. There
are few easy answers to the procurement problem. Most customers (usually referred
to as clients), in terms of absolute numbers, are inexperienced and relatively naïve
in the procurement of construction work but are nonetheless aware of the need to
secure maximum value for money from their construction project. For this group
of clients, the decision to embark on a construction project is probably one of the
most significant financial investments they will ever make. They therefore need to
be considered as a special class of client by those construction professionals charged
with providing them with procurement advice.
At the other end of the scale, the continuing drive by central government, at
least in the UK, towards reinventing the public sector as an educated ‘best
practice’ construction client has led more clients, largely but by no means
exclusively from the public sector, to seek to form increasingly close collaborative
relationships not only with contractors but with the extended supply chain. This
group of clients, who are regular purchasers of building work, are able to use
their enhanced knowledge in conjunction with relatively consistent and sizeable
buying patterns to develop relationships with the supply side and to lever real
advantage through collaboration and strategic alliancing. This enhanced
knowledge base has also led to clients adopting a more discriminating interest in
the quality of the procurement approaches on offer, and this in turn has led to
significant improvements in procurement practice. Nonetheless there are still
further improvements to be made and practitioners continue to have to ‘raise
their game’, in order to convince clients that their advice will genuinely provide
improved value for money.
In terms of the projects on offer, increasing interest in the rehabilitation and
adaptive reuse of historic buildings as an alternative to either demolition or
development on green-field sites gives rise to the need to learn new knowledge

-- 334 of 346 --

Summary 	317
and skills, as is clearly shown by the case study included in Chapter 15, if such
projects are to be judged truly successful.
On the supply side, the landscape is still dominated, at least in terms of
numbers, by small firms, often specialist in character, who focus only upon their
part of the project and usually work as subcontractors to larger firms. Larger
construction firms act as contractors and tend to take on the role of coordinating
these subcontractors. This results in an industry where market conditions can be
very varied and where the majority of customers tend to be disadvantaged
because of their relative inexperience and ignorance of the processes involved in
procurement.
In most cases, investment in a building project is potentially a very high risk,
particularly to this inexperienced majority of clients who seek to procure construc-
tion relatively rarely and on whom the extent of expenditure has a significant
impact in terms of their financial structure. The procurement methodology
developed for the project is increasingly seen as the primary risk management
mechanism for many of the project’s systemic risks.
Real estate has proved to be better than most other forms of investment over
time and the value of an efficient and functional building is significant to most
clients. Saxon (2003) illustrates the enormous multiplying factor where good
design delivers high value.
Government, the industry and its representative bodies have been seeking
performance improvement over the last 20 years and enormous debate has taken
place, with a number of reports and proposals being published. All these have
focused upon process and there is now a wealth of useful advice available to
clients and their project teams, emphasising amongst other aspects the
importance of early integration and the benefits of collaborative procurement
processes in achieving value for money. Nonetheless, despite recent acknowl-
edged improvements in procurement practice, performance of the industry in
terms of benchmarked project delivery continues to suggest a need for still
further improvement.
Construction professionals have historically been perceived by clients to be
rather resistant to change or innovation and there has been a tendency to pursue
traditional practices which are known and well documented, perhaps on the basis
that change is risky, and that ‘it’s best to stick with what you know’. Sadly, in some
pockets of the industry, this continues to be the case even if those practices are
demonstrably less relevant in the market or to the economic conditions that exist at
the time. This book has sought to explore best practice in the context of transition
and has explored both new and existing approaches.
Equally, investment in research and innovation still forms a significantly
smaller proportion of construction output than is the case in other sectors of
the economy, perhaps as little as one tenth of that expended elsewhere, and
mostly emanating from the work of product suppliers or specialist firms rather
than design or construction teams. New products are adopted when confidence
in them is built by some form of certification or when legislation requires
their use.

-- 335 of 346 --

318 	Chapter 16
In this scenario the difficulty associated with achieving the right building
delivered at the right time for the right cost is significant. The key to a successful
outcome rests with the client who must:
■	■ 	Identify the key performance indicators against which project performance can
be judged, consistent with the business case for the project.
■	■ 	Attempt to establish realistic time and cost parameters.
■	■ 	Adopt an appropriate procurement strategy that reflects the above two points
as well as the client’s attitude to risk.
Many clients wrongly assume that they can obtain reliability (defined in terms of
early estimates, programme and functionality) in the purchase of a new building
even though design is incomplete. They rely upon construction professionals to
advise them, and confirm that, as the Construction Clients’ Forum (1998)
expressed, ‘clients do not like unpleasant surprises’.
Those procurement strategies that are most commonly adopted have been
summarised and reviewed, but as Hughes (2004) pointed out, there are so many
potential variables in each case that there is a wide range of amendments which
may need to be made to the standard model.
There is still a tendency for some, generally inexperienced, clients to focus
upon initial capital cost and to ignore both long-term output value and costs
in use. Perhaps this is understandable where clients in the normal course of
their business tend to purchase products which can be seen to be tried and
tested rather than projects which need to be designed. Additionally they have
been less aware of the importance of establishing a bespoke procurement strat-
egy for their project. This has resulted in the dominance of price-led procurement
approaches to both design and the selection of consultants and constructors,
which has not encouraged collaboration or much focus upon a best value
output.
Europe has an increasing influence on UK law and to some extent upon how
things are done. There is no doubt that the UK construction industry can learn
from some of the commonly adopted practices in countries around the world
with large and sophisticated construction industries. This book has summa-
rised some of these practices and particular attention is drawn to the way in
which the interests of clients are protected by mandatory responsibility and
insurance.
Building procurement is a project-based activity using a temporary project team.
The client provides the rationale for the project, initiates the process and is usually
the end user. Wherever possible the client, as the consistent influence throughout
the life of the project, should be empowered to achieve a successful output through
appropriate and timely advice from carefully selected advisers.
It is likely that construction supply will increasingly be provided by ‘fix it’
specialist builders able to employ and train their employees, by smarter builders
focusing on niche markets and by an increase in the development of design and
build joint ventures brought together for larger projects.

-- 336 of 346 --

Summary 	319
Where clients are able to develop an increased awareness of the importance of
their role in the process and a consequential empowerment in terms of setting
project strategy they will be increasingly able to achieve project success in terms of
their own key performance indicators.

Hughes W. (2004) Keynote address. In: Proceedings of the 5th Annual Construction Marketing
Conference, London. Marketing Works Training and Consultancy Ltd.
Saxon R. (2003) Povey Lecture. Joint Contracts Tribunal.

Appendix
Procurement route selection
checklists
These checklists aim to establish information about client needs and the project
under consideration. This information can be further developed, taking into
account the characteristics of various procurement strategies and their associated
risk. Time, design and cost are key criteria in the selection mechanism, as is the
inherent risk of a strategy and its apportionment.
Checklist 1: Time
(1) 	The following questions should be considered:
1.1 	Is completion needed by a specific date?
1.2 	Is completion needed in the shortest possible time?
1.3 	Is the client prepared to pay more for earlier completion?
(2) 	Does the answer to question 1.1 suggest a total project time that is faster than
‘normal’, in the judgment of the client’s advisors?
(3) 	Define the reason for the identified completion or ‘move in’ date:
3.1 	end of lease?
3.2 	sale of premises?
3.3 	new business opportunities?
3.4 	unsuitability of present premises?
3.5 	company restructuring?
3.6 	business milestone?
3.7 	other?
(4) 	Is the need for completion by a specific date or within a specific time frame
more important than:
4.1 	certainty of construction cost before work starts?
4.2 	spending an extended time on design?

-- 338 of 346 --

Procurement route selection checklists 	321
(5) 	What is the approximate value, in sterling, to the client of the building or
facility in terms of contribution, rental or cost savings per month?
(6) 	If the building is completed later than the specified or desired date, will the client:
6.1 	stay in his or her existing premises?
6.2 	find temporary accommodation?
6.3 	close down?
6.4 	adopt some other course?
This evaluation should provide the following information:
■	■ 	Specified completion time.
■	■ 	Reason for completion time.
■	■ 	Whether required completion time is relatively fast.
■	■ 	Whether time is seen as the predominant client need.
■	■ 	The potential financial implications of earlier or later completion.
■	■ 	What action the client may take if the dates are not achieved.
Checklist 2: Design
(1) 	Does the client have clear ideas about the building’s functionality and the
desired design?
(2) 	Does the client have a view about specific building life requirement?
(3) 	Does the site (if selected) pose any particular problems for the designer in
respect of:
3.1 	shape or topography?
3.2 	access?
3.3 	storage space?
(4) 	Does the building type suggest relative design complexity?
(5) 	Does the building type suggest emphasis upon functionality or adaptability?
(6) 	Does the building type suggest highly complex mechanical, electrical or engi-
neering installations?
(7) 	Is it anticipated that extensive changes to design may be required during the
construction phase?
(8) 	Does the client wish to emphasise low running costs?
(9) 	Does the client wish to emphasise low maintenance costs?

-- 339 of 346 --

322 	Appendix
(10) 	Does the client wish to emphasise product quality, at a higher potential cost?
This evaluation should provide the following information:
■	■ 	Whether the client has clear ideas about his or her needs.
■	■ 	Whether the site poses complex design problems.
■	■ 	Whether the building design is complex.
■	■ 	Whether functionality is particularly important.
■	■ 	Whether the client has a long-term view about the building.
Checklist 3: Cost
(1) 	What is the client’s maximum budget?
(2) 	Can the budget be allocated as below:
2.1 	land purchase and fees?
2.2 	construction, including fees?
2.3 	fittings and plant?
2.4 	contingencies?
2.5 	other (define)?
(3) 	Will the client need to have a fixed contract price for the construction element
of the budget or will a reasonably accurate budget be adequate?
(4) 	Will the client need to consider the cost of total building life?
(5) 	What is the client’s willingness to pay more to achieve a quicker completion
date or to obtain higher quality?
This evaluation should provide the following information:
■	■ 	Total maximum spending capacity.
■	■ 	Total construction spending capacity.
■	■ 	Need for consideration of whole life costs.
■	■ 	Need for pre-construction cost certainty.
Analysis
The responses to the checklists should enable the following key factors to be
established:
■	■ 	Whether the project is feasible in terms of time and viable in terms of cost.
■	■ 	If the project is required to be completed by a specified date, whether this is
vital to the project’s success in terms of client needs or whether slippage can be
accommodated if cost is considered.

-- 340 of 346 --

Procurement route selection checklists 	323
■	■ 	If relative speed is required, whether the client can accept less cost certainty and
potentially increased costs.
■	■ 	Whether the project is complex in terms of design or site-related problems.
■	■ 	Whether the need to ensure that the project can be built within a budget is a
priority.
■	■ 	Whether whole life costs need to be considered.
These factors will affect the choice of procurement strategy.
